JOTTINGS BY AN OLD

JOURNALIST.

Saving. "Are you providing silver bullets?" asks the Editor. That it is our duty so to do he forth- with puts beyond controversy. One of his argu- ments reminds me of a saying from the Latin, "No gain is so certain as that which proceeds from the economical use of what you have." Above and beyond all is the appeal to patriot- ism, which, especiAlly plus the 5 per cent. in- terest, may well prove irresistible. Population Question. Last week's "Gossip" pars on this subject brought to my mind several cases of big' families. In one town jn which I spent several years the town clerk and the medical officer of health were each father of 22 children, the first wife surviving in both instances; and I have since met with a similar case elsewhere. I have probably mentiolied before that my maternal grandfather used jokingly to affirm that he had had borne to him three times 13 children, the explanation being that the thirteenth died and was succeeded by another thirteenth, which succumbed, and was replaced in like manner. Lymm''S " Shiriing Light." The loss referred to in the opening paragraph of Lymm "Local facts and fancies" reminded me of a half-century-old joke. As a youth 'living in Fountain Court, London, just 52 years ago, I happened across a cartoon representing a parson rebuking a street boy with, "Don't von know that I am one of the lights of the world?" the retort being, "Then I wish you was hung up in our court, for it's very dark." D.C.M.'s. The reminder as to Lymm's D.C.M. and D.S.O. caused me to turn up an old letter from a former colleague who writes that his town cf 25,000 inhabitants, one native of which adorns it Warrington pulpit, sent to the war a company and a half of Territorials, mostly cotton weavers, the youngest member being 17 and the oldest between 40 and 50. Although unfortun- ately the youngster was killed while acting as volunteer in a desurate enterprise, both these and three other comrades were awarded the D.C.M., and presented by admiring fellow- townsfolk with a valuable gold watch each and other gifts. Of all the amazing feats performed by our brave troops, none have surpassed the Lancashire operatives'.

"Wipe Out the Mouth." I The directions as to treatment of measles re- called an incident of one of my visits to tile Manchester Eye Hospital. I was passing through a ward containing 18 beds when the occupant of one exclaimed "Nor-r-se, nor-r-se, gie ma a bit cloot to wipe ma mooth oot. Ah've had sax teeth oot the mor-r-n, an' Ah canna eat ma meat." I stopped to hav a word with the complaining patient, and I learned that, with a thorn in his eye, the old Scotsman had taken boat to Leith and tramped thence to Manchester, with lifts by the way. His age was 78. The Channel Islands. The letter as to Channel Islanders in War- rington brought me a mental picture of those favourite holiday resorts off the coast of Nor- mandy, which, to the north of the bay of St. Michel, are so irregularly scattered about in the sea as to divide into many narrow channels the inflowing Atlantic, so that the tides vary t very consider bly. When dr equinoctial gales begin to bl , terrific cataracts come dashing over the Castle breakwater, and there is then little hope for vessels that get too close inshore. To the south and west Guernsey's coast runs out to sea in jagged reefs for miles, and the storms that beat about the Hanois lighthouse on the latter extremity are of indescribable severity—while they last, that is, for they are generally of short duration. The linotype opera- tor on one of my old papers left us to take a similar position on the "Guernsey Express" (I think I am right about the title) and he .7 wrote me at length of various experiences. I wonder if any local convalescent soldier from that island knew him. His name was Herbert Dewhirst.

Moon and Weather. Commenting upon a Lymm event previously referred to, "Critic" told us in a recent issue that the clerk of the weather rose to the occa- sion. Has the moon anything at all to do with such matters? I have somewhere read that M. Ilaugergues concluded that/ it had, and the ' eminent French scientist based it upon observa- tions recorded over nineteen years. But when one comes to dip into MI% Henkels' "The Won- ders of the Weather," one finds that authority holding that the moon can scarcely affect the weather; indeed, he's rather nasty about it. Writes he—" Immense masses of statistics have been produced to show the want of connection between moon ' changes ' and weather changes. but the legend still lingers on, perhaps one justification of Carlyle's well-known classifica- tion of the population of these islands." , A competent journalistic friend (modestly sub- mitting it as one of Carlyle's "mostly fools ") made bold to retort that there nevertheless does 'seem to be this connection between, the moon and the weather: that winds have, a habit of coming along "on the back of the ebb," which in itself is a function of the moon's rotation. The rise and fall of the tide over a large area of sea (he went on to say) must necessarily cause a very considerable displacement of 'he superincumbent atmosphere, and in that way it i 4 possible that a fall of tide may tend to produce an indraft of air, not thereby causing a wind, but ,causing a wind which is already on its way to come along more quickly, and on the ebb. So it seems to me to be a case of "as you were."

SILVER BADGE FOR DISCHARGED HEROES. iII thz imintenthltaion oi the Army Council, the King has approved the issue of a Miver badge to officers and men of the Britisli, Indian and Overseas Forces who have served at.home or abroad since August 4, 1914, and who, on account of age or physical infirmity arising from wounds or sickness caused by military service, have, in the ease of officers, retired or relinquished their commissions, or, in the case of men, been discharged from the Army. The badge will also be awarded to members of Queen Alexandra's Imperial Military Nursing Service, Regular, Reserve, and Territorial Forces, Queen Alexandra's Nursing Service for India, and members of Voluntary Aid Detach- mentts who have quitted the service under the above conditions; also to civil practitioners and to other civilians who-, having occupied posi- tions normally held by officers or other ranks of the Royal Army Medical Corps under a fixed agreement for a period of service, have been forced to resign from physical incapacity caused by military sesarice.

===== 0002601_19160729_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
WAR THEMES.

Several articles on war themes in the July number of the "Windsor Magazine" are of timely interest, which is enhanced by the wealth of illustrations accompanying them. Under the title Or "Lords and Commons and their Share in the War," for instance, is grouped a very remarkable series of recent photographs which will have quite an histori- cal value in years to come, and the survey of individual activities which accompanies the pictures presents many facts which. should not be overlooked in the mass of current news which all students of the war must follow. Other articles - incluffe " With an Anti-Aircraft Bat- tery at the Front," "the Fleet of Hospital Ships." The work of the League formed under the auspices of the Y.M.C.A. fbr sending snap- shot photographs from their homes to 'the men at the Front is described in another paper, with details which give special emphasis to the im- portance of preserving as 'far as possible the home ties of our fighting men. War themes of varying/ kinds find dramatic expression In complete stories by Ethel Turner, Vincent Brown and C. M. Matheson. Other contribu- tors of remarkable stories are Fred M. White. E. M. Jameson, and Theodore Goodridge Roberts, and this wealth of 'fiction, grave'and gay, is attractively illustrated by such dis- tinguished artists as Fred Pegrani, G. C. Wilms- hurst, Maurice Greiffenhagen, Harold Copping, Cyprus Cnneo, and others almost equally well known.

Satisfied. A man of an inquiring turn of Mind thrust his finger into a horse's mouth to see how many teeth it had, and the horse closed its mouth to -see how many fingers the man had. be curiosity of each w)ts fully satisfied.

WOUNDED.

COLLAPSED ON DISCHARGE.

New Zealander Who Was To Have

Been Married In Warrington. • - Trooper William Evans Thomas, of Auck- land, New Zealand, who left New Zealand as a member of the 3rd Auckland Mounted Rifles, was wounded by shrapnel in the Gallipoli fighting and was sent to England and quar- tered at the Lord Derby Hospital. He had many friends in Warrington, including Mr. and Mrs. Renwick, formerly of New Zealand, and now residing in Warrington. He retqrned to New Zealand convalescent and intended to come back to England shortly to marry Miss Travis, 110, Sharp-street, but he collapsed in the street after having been 'discharged as cured and died in hospital. Trooper Thomas was accorded a military funeral at the Waiku- mete Cemetery. A gun carriage was provided by "A" Battery, New Zealand Field Artillery and a firing party by the Mount Eden Guard. Returned soldiers attending the funeral wore uniform. • , • - • • •

FOUGHT AT MONS.

Warrinfitonian Complains Of Small

-F'urlough Allowance.

Driver T. O'Donnell, R.F.A., a reservist, who has been in the thick of the fighting almost continuously since the beginning of the war, arrived in Warrington this week on a seven days' furlough. He complains bitterly of the meagre allowance that is made to the men who have been fighting for their country. .In his case he was given 235. at Woolwich. and out 'of this he had to pay 16s. id. for railway fare to Warrington. He is a single man without home, and is puzzled by the task of keeping himsPif for 4 week on the balance of 6s. 11d., especially n ben the commodities of life are so dear. He thinks it unfair that a Canadian or kustralia a should receive 7s. 6d. a day and the English "Tommy" cnlv Is. a-day. O'Donnell was in the retreat From Mons and the victory on the Marne, and was gassed sub- sequently at La Basse. After a stay in an Eng- lit± hospital he returned ,to the Front in Dttember, 1915, and .has seen. some deadly -fighting at close quarters in the Ypres region. He has a sister residing at CI, Foundry-street. and another at 32, Battersby lane. Formerly he worked at Bewsey Forge.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JULY 29, 1916.

WOUNDED AND MISSING.

THE BROTHERS LAVVSON.

===== 0002601_19160729_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
One Wounded And The Other Missing After Advance.

As a result of the recent heavy fighting in France, the two soldier-sons of Mr. and Mrs. E. Lawson, 54, Buttermarket-street, have met with mishaps. Private Joseph -Lawson, the younger son, was wounded on, the P3rd instant, and has since been brought to the Cambridge Hospital, Aldershot. Here, he has been visited by his mother, who found him suffering from a bullet wound in the arm. It was he who disclosed to his mother the news that the elder son, Sergeant Charles Lawson, was missing since the 3rd instant. They are both attached to the Border Regiment, and had been serving at the Front together for twelve months. They enlisted in September, 1914, and are old

===== 0002601_19160729_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
CORPORAL J. ROBERTS.

Wounded By Shell %Midi Kills Several Comrades.

After serving during the Dardanelles cam- paign and being invalided home with frost-bite and rheumatism, Corporal J. Roberts, of the Border Regiment, and son of Mr. and Mrs. R. Roberts, 154, Winwick-road, has been wounded in France, receiving a shrapnel wound in the back. • ,The shell which wounded him 'killed several others. Corporal Roberts, who is 22 years of age, was formerly employed by the London and North-Western Railway Co. He was educated at Heathside School.

===== 0002601_19160729_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE E. HITCHINSON.

Battle That Knocked Loos Into A "Cocked Hat."

One of the four ,patriotic. sons of Mr. and Mrs. V. Hitchinson, of 67, Priestley-street, has been wounded, in the person of Private Edward Hitehinson, of the Machine Gun Corps. Writing from a Brighton hospital to his parents he says :—" I have been wounded, but it is not very much. It is a shrapnel wound in the lower part of the body, and it causes me a lot of ,pain. But I will be all, right soon, and it won't be long befote I 'come home. I have got three souvenirs from the Germans, I sup- pose you have read about the carryings on at Wood. That was where I was, and some of the felloi\rs who were at Loos said the battle for this place knocked Loos 'into a cocked hat.'" _ Private Hitchinson, who is 18 years of age, was called up in his group in December, and had been at the Front three weeks. He was formerly employed as a wire worker at the Whitecross works, and was educated at the Heathside School. ' Of the other brothers, Private Arthur Hollins Hiichinson, of the South Lancs. Regi- ment, was reported wounded and missing on the

10th August, 1915, and nothing has since been heard of him. He was 19 years of age. Seaman Herbert Hitchinson, who is dilly 17 years of age, has been in the Navy three months. John James, the eldest son, joined the Ride Brigade in the early days of the war, but was discharged after a few weeks' service owing to deafness. He tried to enlist several times afterwards, but was not accepted, and is now on munition work in the Midlands. He is 21 years of age.

===== 0002601_19160729_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
GUNNER C. FELLOWS.

In Hospital At .Bath Mt Shrapnel In

His Hip.

Gunner Charles Fellows, of the South Lancs. Regiment, whose home address is 10, Back Atherton-street, Bank Quay, was wounded- in action on June 6th. Writing from a hospital at Bath he says he was struck with shrapnel in the right hip. • Gunner Fellows, who is 34 years of age, is the son of Mr. and Mrs. Ambrose Fellows, who have five sons and two sons-in-law serving in the Army. He was formerly employed on the cement plant at Messrs. JOs. ' Grosfield and Sons' works, where he had been for 15 years. He enlisted shortly after the outbreak of war, and has been at the Front thirteen months. He has four children. He was educated at the National School, Church-street.

===== 0002601_19160729_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
CORPORAL E. H. JONES.

" Examiner " Reached Late German Trenches by July 2nd. _

_ Corporal E. H. Jones, Royal Fusiliers '(Public Schools) son of Mr. E. Wynne Jones, Late-Il- ford, was wounded in the neck on Saturday, July 15th by shrapnel. ,After spending four days in the base hospital he was transferred to Dublin Castle Military Hospital. He was in the original charge on July Ist. He and his comrades" went over the parapet at 7.30 in the morning and succeeded in promptly clipturing the German trenches and taking its occupants prisoners. The Germans, he says, were "dead beat," our artillery having cut off their sup- plies for some time previously. We consoli- dated our position in the captured trenches, and on the following day, "The Warrington

polisher at Messrs. Greenhalgh's works, and Later worked for various firms.

PRIVATE WILLIAM RICHARDSON.

Bowled The Germans Over• As They

Ran Away.

Private William Richardson, Rifle Brigade,l a furnaceman employed by the Richmond Gas Stove Co., who lived with his mother at 113, , Wellington-street, has been wounded during the advanee. Fortunately the injuries were not severe, and on Monday his mother re- ceived a letter stating that he had already returned to the firing line. In,his first letter sending news of his wound, Private Richardson said :—" I got a small shrapnel wound at the -back of my right knee and I put my left knee Out again.. We went over in the morning and it was half-past eleven at night before I could get from the German lines. Young Taylor had half his left arm blown away. P. bandaged him up about half- past eight at night. . . Talk about Aubers Ridge! It was nothing to this. I 'made good use .of my bayonet and ,not half! ,As the Ger- mans were running away I was bowling them

over." Private Richardson • was I in the battle of Neuire Chappelle and many ,other engage- ments. , AuVerS Ridge was the place at which ,he *as wounded last May. He re- turned to the Front on New Year's, Day. His father was -the late Mr. John Richatdsoxi, wire- drawer at Whitecross, and a "prominent Con- servative Club worker. Private Richardson; who is 33 years of age and single, is an old Wycliffe Day School boy and attended Wycliffe Church. Charles Henry Richardson, his brother, is a Sergeant in the Grenadier Guards, which he I joined in 1898. Having served in the Boer War he joined the Cheshire and then the Stafford- shire ConsObulary, and was called to the Colours as a reservist when the present war was deelared. Tie was wounded at the battle of Loos. He is 36, married, and has six children, I who are staying with his mother in Wellington= street. He belongs to the St. John Ambulance Brigade, and possesses a life saving certificate.

PTOMAINE POISONING.

Painter's Death Probably Due To Eating Brawn.

Having heard eVidence concerning the death of David Dunn (35), painter and decorator, 304, Liverpool-road, Warrington,, a Coroner's jury found that the same was due' to gastro-enteritis, caused by Ptomaine poisoning, which, in turn, was probably set up by eating bi:awn. The man died last Saturday after eight days' ill-

ness. • Mr. S. Brighouse, connty coroner, held his inquiry .on Tuesday, and Mr. W. S. Kntwles (solicitor) represented Mr. and Mrs. Arthur Keats, 2, Edwin-street, Widnes, at whose sh 4 the brawn in qUestion as said to have been bought. Mrs. Dunn, wife of deceased, said that on the 13th in§t., she. and her husband, a fairly healthy man, went to visit ber sister. Mrs. Warren, 92, Peel House-lane, Widnes. On the following day they had dinner at 1.30, stewed steak, haricot beans, potatoes and black cur- rant tart, and went out for a walk. Returning at five o'clock they had tea and some brawn bought from Mrs. Keats. Mr. Dunn ate the greater. portion of the brawn, and said he enjoyed it very much. They had a supper of Ipotatoes, peas and bread and butter. At mid- night her husband complained of pains, and was seized with diarrhoea, and sickness. :Warren and hertson were also taken seriously ill and Dr. McKenzie was called in. Witness took her husband home in a taxi and gave him castor oil and Indian brandy. He did not im- prove, and Dr. Gandhi 'WAS called in aud at- tended him until his :death. Witness said that only half a pound of brawn, which was home- made, was bought. She felt ill and had nothing

Ittttas to' the Obitur

PROHIBITION SUNDAY.

Sir,—The sermons preached on "Prohibition ound °Y and reported in Your current issue are really most unconvincing. Many clergymen appear to be simply 'overawed by teetotalist ter- rorismk; their evident lack of knowledge of the subject 'shows very clearly that they are Ex- Pressing not the conviction of their own reason,. but views forced upon them by outside pressure. r-rhe Rev. WI T. Derry, for instance, said "He had always regarded prohibition as an imprac, ticable dream for this country, but its tremen- dous progress in Canada, and especially in the United States of America, had quite altered his °Pinion." The adoption of prohibition by cer- tain Canadian provinces is too recent for any conclusion to be drawn as to its success or other- wise, there is no data upon which to form ny Opinion, so this can scarcely have influenced Mr. Derry's views; nor is it possible ta‘see how these could have been altered in favour' of pro- hibition by the exverience of the United States. Everyone who has visited the so-called " dry " States declares prohibition to be a -failure and a fraud. Where drinking has been technically " abolished " we find abounding drunkenness— more drunkenness in proportion to population, in fact, than many of the "wet" States. Mr. Charles Stirrup, American correspondent of the "Sunday Chronicle," has an illuminat- ing article in last. Sunday's issue on this very subject. He states that prohibition in America has been reduced to a farce, for the simple reason that public opinion is really against the Liquor- Law, and ' the authorities are conse- quently unable to enforce it. In Atlanta, Ga.. a supposed prohibition town, there are 2,200 liquor saloons doing an illegal trade quite Openly! The State of Maine, which was tlie first in the linion to adopt compulsory teetotalism, does not appear to be altogether satisfied with its 70 Years' experience of prohibition, for the last time its citizens had an opportunity of voting Oh the question the law was only retained by a Majority of 758 in a total poll of 120,000! I think if the Rev. W. T. Derry would ex- amine the easily-available evidence, he would hesitate to recommend to Great Britain a hypo- critical and futile system of repression which has proved so useless in America. J. E. SIMPSON. Birkenhead.

CAPTURING CHILDREN.

Sons Of Temperance Out To

Throttle Drink Traffic.

• Having in view the object of preserving chil- dren against the temptations of alcoholic drink, the Order of the Sons of Temperance held a cadet campaign meeting in the Bewsey- road Wesleyan Lecture Hall on Monday even- ing for the purpose of obtaining fresh members. Bro. J. McGregor Mclntyre, national division organiser, visited Warrington for the occasion, and delivered a very interesi.:ng address. Bro. It- Jones, W.P., presided over the meeting, and Was supported by Bros. Clare, G.w.T., and Wardman, W.S. In the course of his opening remarks the Chairman said the campaign was one which Was being carried on extensively throughout the Country. There was a; diversity of opinion with regard to the future of the drink traffic, some temperance advocates being in favour of total DrOhibition, and others in favour of nationalisa- tion by State purchase. These opinions mat- tered little to him (the speaker. The object he had in view was the throttling of the drink traffic, and one of the best means to this end 'was the capturing of the child, and through the cadet section, • instilling into it the prin- ciple of total abstinence.

===== 0002601_19160729_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
Great Lives Shattered.

in opening his address, Bro. Mclntyre said the drink traffic was a discord in the national life, and, referring particularly to musicians, said the lives of many such people had been shattered by strong drink. They were out Chiefly for the children, for they must be looked after for the,welfare of the country. They were out to make children total abstainers, not be- cause the elder people were such, but to pro- tect them against the great difficulties which they would have to face when they grew older. That Order had a total membership of ,256,000, the adult branches having increased last year by 5,000, while 6,422 were transferred fl:om the juvenile to the adult sections. Ninety- /line per cent. of this number had remained true to the pledge, while out of a State mem- bership of 132,000, only nine had broken the Pledge. Forty thousand members were serving their King and country, including a great many Officials. Two members had won the V.C. Musical items were rendered during trhe .evening by a male voice choir (conducted by Mr. J. Hannan), and also by Miss E. Tanner.

===== 0002601_19160729_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
Cburtly Arius,

WHEN THE BOYS COME HOME.

Rev. T. T. James And The Questions

That Will Be Asked.

The Rev. T. T. James, preaching the last of his series of sermons at Wycliffe Church on "The Church and the War," last' Sunday night, spoke on "The Church and the Welcome Home."

===== 0002601_19160729_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
The Joy of Home-coming.

"This conflict," he said, "is not over. We shall commit grave and serious error if we relax any effort we can make here at home to strengthen and increase our resources. If, for instance, anyone imagines that we have eout so far in this struggle that any additional drastic restriction of the liquor traffic is unnecessary, and that the suggestion for it comes late in the. day, it may be just as well to disabuse our minds of .such conceptions. Now if ever in this great struggle of the nations our national life needs the most serious handling. But all that need not prevent us speaking of the home- coming. It is something we are looking for- ward to. The lads out yonder are looking forward to it, and in every city and village and hamlet in this country there are hearts for whom the days are long, long days while they wait for it. There is a gladness in our hearts when we think of it, though we can never for- get that all our gladness is touched by sorrow. We hav'e incurred too great a cost in this thing for any mere empty and vulgar demonstration, too great a cost in our bravest and best loved; we shall be glad with a joy that has paid a price when we see those who went forth from us in our midst again." An Englishman's Inherent Love of Home.

As to the relation of the Church to the home- corners, there were those who got into a panic of 'uncertainty because of the changed condi- tions that had been brought about by the con- tact of so many with foreign countries and strange surroundings. "There ,is another side," said Mr. James, "which is often enough lost sight of, and it is the tremendous affection a man has for his own land, and the ineradicable feeling an English- man at any rate possesses that, see- what he may abroad, we after all do things very much better in this country. And we shall do well to 'remember that while many strange experi- ences make a difference, there will also be the longing to re-possess the old things which have been surrendered for a time. I speak of what I know when I say that out there often enough our lads are just longing for the old street and the old home, and many of them for the old church and the old Sunday school and the old Sunday, and we need not fear too much that we shall find them transformed beyond recognition in their habits 'and their, ways."

But in saying this he did not mean that we should discover that nothing had happened to these men. As a Church they would have four classes to deal with: those who formerly had no religious belief and still had none; those who had a real religious faith that had stood tb e stress of temptation peculiar to camp life and would return with deepened and strengthened faith; those to whom the war has been a moral and spiritual disaster because they had not been strong enough to resist subtle and un- usual forms of temptation; and those who in considerable numbers have been re-awakened to religious experience by the sacrifice made for a great cause. Questions to be Faced.

"I think," said Mr.-. James, "that we may look upon this last class as something from which real encouragement is to be derived. It is essential that we should ask what thoSe who return will look for in the Church of Jesus Christ, and what the Church must offer them. Perhaps it is possible to 'sum up the -Thole situation by saying that supremely the Charch will need to possess the note of reality. For we must realise how there will be something vitally- different about those who have been through the fire of this crisis. It will not be so much a difference of externals, but a difference of experience. The simple fact is that there will be boys of twenty in our midst who will have gone through an experience which will never come to some of us if we live to be a hundred. We take the tragedy of life in small doses, and largely by hearsay. They will have stood in the midst of it, and- will have crowded' more into a few short months than we get into a lifetime. 'Those who come back to us will demand above everything else reality in our religious life, in our religious faith and belief. I do not honestly think they will be sufficiently interested in the mere names of churches, or the Mere.. forms of worship, or the mere machinery of it, to criticise these things to a great extent, but they Will be interested, where their minds are turned to religion, in the reality of it. It is the fundamental religious questions which our lads are facing out yonder; whether God really rules The world where war is waged; whether Divine Providence exists ;in a world where bullets seem to pick out men by chance, and luck; whether, where life seems so cheap, ,and men are moved about like pieces on a board, it can be said that every single indi- vidual life is of value in God's sight; whether, when a man is put out, there is anything be- sides his body and' the rough cross that Stands above it; whether it is possible to believe in the Father Heart of a univeise where multi- tudes are permanently maimed and disfigured; whether war like this is the last word of the Church or of Christ Himself about interna- tional relationships. Men are not asking •:liese questions as they may be asked in a debating society', or in merely speculative conversation, but because they are living in the midst of grew realities. And any Church or any religion which is playing about with makebelieve, which is not concerned with deep and fundamental I questions, which has not the ring of reality, 'will I not do for those who have been in the midst of I realities themselves."

===== 0002601_19160729_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
BOLD STREET WESLEYANS' WALKING DAY.

Bold-street Wesleyans had their field day last Saturday. Following on similar lines to those of last year they did not go anything like so far afield as has been their custom in former years, being content with a field at Stockton Heath. It must at once be said that this detracted noth- ing from the children's enjoyment; in fact,- it added to if, because being nearer home, they were enabled to spend a maximum amount of time in the open air, without wasting, any ,time in railway journeys. Leaving Palmyra-square by special car at ,2 o'clock they soon arrived Nat .a field off Whitefield-road, kindly lent by Mr. Williams, and at once began to amuse them- selves with football, cricket, roundervskipPing and various other games. Everything Was splen- didly organised, the carrying out of the arrange- ments leaving nothing to be desired', and both officials and teachers are to be congratulated, and particularly on the good terms on which they must be with the Clerk of the weather, for the sun'shone brilliantly all the time. At half- past four an adjournment was made for tea, Wesleyan friends at Stockton Heath very kindly lending their schoolroom for this purpose. Tea over, games were again indulged in, and when the air becarpe cooler the races were run, these providing same exciting finishes. The officials were very pleased to see present so many of the parents and friends of the children, who, it may he said, enjoyed themselves quite as much as the children themselves, only less boisterously, of course. The time passed only too quickly, and when the return journey was begun at half-past eight, again by special car, the chil- dren—tired but happy—seemed somewhat loth to leave. . „

70,000 FLAGS.

£370 Raised For Or Allies'

H ospitals.

Saturday last was the occas of a big egert in the 6use of our Allies, Russia, l'rance and Italy, in and about Warrington. Hundred; ladies, daintily garbed, were busily engaged selling miniature flags 4 rom early morn to dewy eve, with the result that everyone who walked abroad wore one or other of the pretty decorations. In fact, many people, particularly those of the sterner sex, displayed quite a cordon of flags, either round their hats or on their coat-lapels: Quite a number of ladies were out as early as five o'clock to capture travellers by the early cars. , Good business was done in the suburban districts. The military hospitals of our heroic allies must have benefited largely by Saturday's collections. In Warrington and district over 70,000 flags were sold, and some- thing like £370 realised. The arrangements were perfect, there being an executive com- mittee, and certain ladies organised each ward and suburb. .The flags were displayed' in boxes covered with blue, white and red paper, and decorated with similarly coloured ribbons: The boxes were kindly presented by Mr. Felix Maginn, of the Alliance Box Company, while the collecting boxes had been formerly used on behalf of the Infirmary Million Penny Fund. The Mayor (Alderman Peacock) was kind enough to provide light refreshments in the Town Hall for the workers. It should be mentioned that Allies Day was the first public function in which the baby daughter of the Mayor and Mayoress, then a week old, has taken part. Board Of Guardians' Persiflage Regard- ing A Major's Caligimphy.

Macaroni With Tomato Sauce. Boil one quarter of a pound of macaroni in plenty of boiling, salted water for .twenty minutes or until tender. Drain in a colander, then throw in cold water for five minutes to blanch. Drain again. Stew one pint of tomatoes for fifteen minutes, then press through a fine sieve. Melt one tablespoonful of butter without browning. A'dd to it two tablespoonfuls of flour, mix until smooth; then add the tomatoes and stir until it thickens; season with salt and pepper; add the macaroni, stir gently until heated, and serve. To Reduce Fat.

All sorts of reclucingoystems have been in vogue, first and last, but as they were based on Mistaken —ideas of what happened to food 'materials in the chemical laboratory of the huthin body,. they have not been generally satisfactory. Recently, we have , learned that the best way to grow thin is not to fast, but to eat. Bulky,plaxative food is the thing. Un- .cooked bran is a bulky -laxative, and two or three tablespoonfuls should be eaten at each meal. Lettuce, spinach, celery, kale, Onions, cabbage, chard, radishes—all the green, succu- lent vegetables may be eaten in quantities, two or three at each meal. Fresh fruits of all kinds are allowed. In case any special fruit or vege- table disagrees with an indivi4ual, he must avoid that, of course. Zwieback and bran bread toast may be eaten in. small amounts, and the pure gluten bread sticks, or biscuit, as one chooses. Very little butter, and no cake, sweets or puddings are .prescribed. An abundance of pure water to flush the kidneys is desirable, but not with the meals. No salt should be eaten, and lemon juice may be taken on vege- tables when an acid is desired. Exercise is a great factor in reducing weight, and walking is the best exercise. Hard hand massage, mechanical massage, and automatic electrical machine exercise are all great aids in reducing if one can have them. Abdominal fomentations, leg packs, wet sheet packs, and sweating baths not too prolonged- WEI aid in "melting" the flesh. There should be a gain in muscular strength accompanying the loss of flesh. The patient should have more nervous energy and a feeling nf inerensing strength while reducing. —"Good Health."

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

The Chelsea' Hospital for Women.

The month of July has seen the opening by the Queen of two hospitals in London for the benefit of women. The South London Hospital in Clapham Common has been built mainly by contributions from women, and the Chelsea Hospital for Women, opened by Her Majesty a few days later, had a generous friend in the late Earl Cadogan, who presented' the site, which has been valued at over twenty thousand pounds. This hospital, which was founded in 1871, for the treatment of ,women's diseases, was not intended exclusively for the poorest, classes, but benefited also sufferers of small means .to whom the fees of a nursing home were pio- hibitive. Small wards for such patients proved a great boon, and the system has been folloWed in the new buildings. Queen Alexandra, when Princess of Wales, laid the foundation stone of the original building. ' . Women's Volunteer Reserve. •

Perhaps no feminine organisation has proved more serviceable to the country since conscrip! tion became a -necessity, than the Women's Volunteer. Reserve, whose members have in- creased their activities and filled the places of men in most unexpected places. Their work covers a wide field, some of the volunteers hold- ing, the position of heads of attestation offices, as in Birmingham, others are acting as instruc- tors on farms, and yet others form air raid squads in London, and various parts of the country. Every week the activities, of the Reserve develop, and work increases, therefore new members are badly .needed as hedpers. More especially in the canteens where meals are provided night and day for soldiers and munition workers. Motor drivers for the ambulances are also sought. The Reserve has now forty country brafiches, and over ten thousand menibers in Britain, whilst Van- couver boasts four companies. The idea of the organisation originated with the Hon. Evelina Haverfield. who did splendid work with the Scottish Women's Hospital in Serbia.

Black Hats and Matinee Toques. The black hat is as popular as the black gown, and is most becoming in a big shape when given a brim of lace and a crown of chiffon velvet.. Frequently the crown is transparent, revealing the coiffure, but this mode appeals only to the few, hence the revival of the soft black crown. When black net or tulle forms the brim, effect is sought in silver lace, veiled with' the diaphanous =Aerial, and resting on a trans- parent brim. A new type 9f summer hat for wearing with river or country cotton frocks is made of plaited linen, the same colour as. the gown. The shapes preferred are of the small variety, fitting well on the head. A very pretty matinee toque is shown which is low in the

OPINIONS ON THE GREAT ADVANCE.

MY DEAR CHILDREN,

have received from you a Very intelligent set of opinions on the great British and French advance against the Germans. It has been most difficult to decide who was the best, but, all things considered, I have placed first

HIGHLY COMMENDED.

Niece Maud A. Lunny, 108 a, Bridge-street (Arpley-street).--" The advance must have been ,terrible. I can just fancy the noise of the guns and the roaring of the big heavy cannons. I can also imagine the dead bodies lying in heaps, and the wounded yelling with pain, and some nearly going mad; the enemy pleading for their liyee to the English and making excuses to spare them. It must be _awful to see the 'carriers' who at night carry the sick and wounded to hospitals, and then in the morning see them perhaps bringing them to Warrington in the motor ambulances on stretchers; and to see the poor wounded horses that cannot tell where the terrible pain is rolling about from right to left."

Nephew Clarence Ou!tram, \57, Willis-street (Parochial) says we have imProired our position very much, and if we steadily keep on as we are doing at present the war will soon be over, and, of course, that is ,what everybody wants. We all know this is the worst part of the war, and ,to turn back now would me,an England's downfall. We should be under German rule, and that would neverf do. Niece Norah Calleghan, 152, Wellfield-street (Sacred I leart),,says the advance has given us courage to endure. When the good news came even the little newspaper boys,had a smile that wouldn't cohie off. ‘,‘,Aly opinion, is that we have come to the toughest part of the war, and we have the satisfaction of knowing that our brave 'Pommies ' are at their very best and the jolly Jack Tars are striving their utmost to win 'The Day.' May good, luck follow them in every 'attack as it did in the great advance in France !"

Nephew Fred. Rathbone, 112, Algernon-street (Parochial), is of opinion that this big advance will prove the beginning of the end of this great war. The French are doing very well in what he thinks is the greatest battle that was ever fought in France.

_ Nephew Edwin Gandy, 64, Back Dallam-lane, thinks the advance of our soldiers a splendid niece of work. It was a pretty hard struggle, but we pulled through. It is not only. the soldiers wlin have done somet4ing, but the munition workers. "I hope they send muni- tions more and more, then the soldiers will be able to advance."

Nephew Harold Freaney, 107, Cartwright- street, (Sacred Heart), thinks the war has only just begun—as Lohl Kitchener said it would— and that we are "having the best of it." Nephew Frank Overend, 74, Lythgoes-lane (Hamilton-street), says the victory is one that will live in history. The advance will be re- membered by those who took part in it—such as those shown in "The Examiner" portraits; it will also urge young soldiers to do brave deeds so as to be honoured and promoted. Nephew James Ryder, 46, Elizabeth-street (St. Barnabas').—" The great advance is one o4' the most striking and dramatic events of the great European War. I think it will nearly finish the-war, because the Germans have lost nearly all their soldiers and nearly all their colonies. All the British and the Allies' sol-

(hers who have fallen in the great advance will be thought of for ever." Niece Elsie Johnson, 323, Lovely-lane (Wycliffe), says that we have lost a great many men of vigorous build, but our motto is "To do or to die." She hopes the war will 'bear great fruit. Nephew Ernest W. Sankey, 99, Kimberley- street (St. Barnabas').—" If the British and French keep on they will soon- beat the Ger- mans back to their frontier, and the Germans will fight like savage men, not civilised men. The advance is largely due to us having plenty of shells and ammunition. The enemy are getting more weary every day, as is reported in a paper taken from a captured German officer's document. I think the enemy will ,be utterly beaten in about eighteen months, the way things are going on." Niece Edith Pugh, 17, Ship-street, Brecon, South Wales.—" I think that we all at home should do •otir ,utmost for our valiant men who have done such great work at .the Front. By the papers the British have been going through hard 4hting, and have been capturing vil- lages, also miles_ of trenches,_ and so gaining

_ ground all the time. It is also wonderful to hear that the brave. Tommies' go to their work without a murmur, and how they are so ready to face death."

Niece Evelyn Mason, 8, Frederick-street, Latchford (Bolton) says the British Army must have some very noble, commanders, and by their splendid work the "boys" are of brave and courageous hearts. The Kaiser has been boasting abut his large army, but a man like the Kaiser is not to be believed, as we all have found.

Nephew Edgar Lehrle, 194, Battersby-lane (Beamont), says the great advance is a deed that will be long remembered in English his- tory. Now that we have the enemy on .the run it will enable us to secure France and Belgium. It must be a great honour to those who have fought ,in it. Nephew Harry 'Heywood, 8, Matthews-street (Parochial School), is 'of opinion that the advance has been a great success for the English soldiers and their leader, Douglas Haig, a brave and noble hero. "I think the advance will be over in a few more months; the Germans will be forced to retreat to their own country." Nephew Dennis Farrow, 46, Willis-street, thinks more soldiers have been killed or wounded in this advance than in any other battle in the war. When the news of this big advance reaches the Russian soldiers it will liven them up and they will get farther into Austria. The first thing that will be said in the German Parliapent after the advance is: "Let us put the war to an end." The other way is to advance until we get to Berlin and put an end to it ourselves. ' Niece Doris Appleton, 20, Portland-street (Wycliffe) says that by the great " push " we have cleared the way for other and greater things. "I hope this war will soon be over and that we shall still be waving the old flag, Union Jack."

YOUTHFUL WIRELESS INVENTOR.

There is a 17-year-old boy, living in Illinois, who has made quite a reputation by his skill and workmanship as a wireless inventor. The "American Boy" says that this boy has been experimenting with wireless for several years. During that time he has perfected a number of novel methods for exhibiting the utility of this wonderful force. One of his stunts is the sink- ing of a battleship by wireless at a distance of 200 feet. A trick, of course. The battleship is a miniature reproduction, •cunningly and cleverly reconstructed of 24 different pieces, which fall apart When the wireless is brought into effect.

Another clever device which the boy built is ship which is steered and controlled by wire- less from a distance of 1,000 feet. The boat built by the young expert for this experiment is 31 feet long, with a displacement of' 35th It is ;provided with a motor for its propuls:on, and reaches a 'speed of seven Miles an hour. On one occasion the boy expert gave a talk on "The How and Why of Wireless," illus- trating it with practical demonstrations.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JULY 29, 1916

ARE WE AT OUR FULL STRENGTH?

We are rapidly approaching the end of ourf, second year sof War, and we have the humilia- tion of seeing a memorial sent forth bs, many men and women of public distinction appeal- ing, in words of fire, to the Gorernment to put forth the whole strength of the nation in this fight for the purity of our homes and the libey of the world.. Our whole str.migth T'.es it not seem like a mcrdtery to ask siith a question? Our whole strength? Have we not reversed the Biblical anticipation. •lf the good time coming and literally turned our ploughshares and pruning hooks intO swords? Is not every "'works in the country diverted from the manufacture of useful articles to the turning out of instruments of death? Is not our overseas c9mmerce crippled to collap-iing point in order that men and munitions may be carried from and to the uttermost erds of the earth to come under the spell and influ- ence of the battle cries on the largest, the bloodiest field of strife that the world has ever known? Are we not all striving with bated breath to put the last ounce of our strength into the scales against the foe—that foe which was once within a few miles of Paris, and, therefore, not far removed from intrading England? Our whole strength? Have not our banks and great financial houses plured forth their millioffs and their hundreds of millions to pay theArernendous cost of tpis *feat and long maintained effort to' save the Empire, our Allies, and civilisation? Have we not even come to the working classes and asked them to save their sixpences that silver bullets may not cease to flow for the discom- fiture and overthrow of the Hun hordes?

Still we are asked to give our whole strength and put the issue beyond eoubt. Where are we falling short? In which respeet are we failing to do ourselves and our great cause justice? These distinguished men and women tell us. They disclaim being temper- ance reformers, but they Support. the deniand for prohibition "made to the Government by its own investigators and by the shipbuilders' deputation with not a teetotaler airong them." The PRIME MINISTER has said that "no sacrifice%is too great when freedom and honour are at stake.' But we refuse to sacrifice our drink. France and .11us3la nade this one great sacrifice almost spontaneously. The KING and LORD KITCHENER set the ex- ample to this country, but to no avail. The memorialists believe the country, recognising the present golden opportunity, would Fiske the sacrifice and would agree to tire suspen- sion of the liquor traffic during the war. They further believe that the conversion of the public-houses into houses of refreshment' "will quicken up our fighting populetions, will raise a new fire of resolution ;T. on:- people and will give to millions the first oppor- tunity they have ever had of 'breaking old habits of weakness and forming new hahits of strength."

That is an ideal. What are the Nets in practice? While we are soliciting sixpences for war savings certificates we are wasting £180,000,000 a year on drink, or a.lout £20,000,000 more than we'spent in the re.lord days in peace time, when all our men were at home. The spending of this precious :nonel is not the worst of the waste it entails. The effect of the drink has interfered with the efficiency of the Army, the Navy, an.] the munition works, especially in the early tinges of the war; its manufacture has used up the energy of many workers, and its transport. has occupied ships and trains and horses—and the time of the men attending them—that could have been used to better advantage; the traffic has used up material that could ive been used for food; it has called for the pro- duce of a million acres of land; and during the war, so far, it has caused the consumption of 3,000,000 tons of coal. Are we"at our full strength while we are dissipating it in ;iris rude fashion?

In these circumstances the response that has been made to the prohibition petition, which has rested in our churches for the past fortnight, has been peculiarly feeble. We have endeavoured to ascertain the result, but have not been particularly successiul. Alto- gether about 1,600 have signed tiro various petitions. Among the .clergyr-en and minis- ters who have done so, so far as we 'an ascer- tain, are the Rector (Canon Wills), and the Revs. T. T. James, W. T. Derry, H. Ogden and A. J. Morton—besides all th) otters of the Working Men's Mission, and Councillor Henshall, of the Grappenhall Independent Methodist Church. Details of signat,res in- clude: Working Men's Mission, 400; Besvmay- road Wesleyan, 108; l'adgate Wesieyan, .90; Wycliffe Congregational, 80; Grapporilnei Independent Methodist, 80; GolborTie-street Baptist, 60. The petitions will remkin at the churches for a few days longer. Shall wo make an effort to get into the fight at our full fighting strength?.

I have heard any expressions of regret at the fact that a- bublic redeptioix. eras not 'given to COlonel Crosfield on his arrtv)ii honie. But Colonel Crosheld .willed it Lth.ir.

We are all jubilant at his return. Yes'..-hrtlay, on visiting the works at Bank Quay, Le was given a hero's welcome.

Before his arrival the office staff were told that they could cheer, but must not jam the corridors. When he appeared, however, there was a• general rusli to see him, and, the cheers loud and sincere.

The ringing of a fire bell or the coming of a storm will always drain main streets of their crowds. On Wednesday night the suspending of Niagara Falls over Warrington scattered those taking an airing. .

Many who were drenched -aver that it wa.4 the heaviest rainfall we have had for years. One on a Cemetery tramcar at the time was heard to say: "I've been on this car when it has jumped and rolled and now it's taken to swimming."

They say the water was at least two feet deep in Church-street. I don't want to throw a wet blanket on the story, but it reminds one of Mr. Roosevelt's River Doubt!

Acting Sergeant Newey is retiring from the I'olice Farce on a pension after 25 years' ser- vice. Everyone knows him, and everyone will wish him well. Everyone will also wish he (that's all of us) were retiring, too.

The Cemetery Committee are recommending to the Town Council meeting on Tuesday, the appointment of Mr. A. Hallows as temporary grave digger. He will mobably bury many men without halos.

Early this week the Warrington Football Chia flag drooped drearily at half-mast, mourning the death of George Thomas, hero of many a well-fought field. It looked war-worn and tattered, just as it might have waved over the gallant soldier in our "far flung battle line."

The Mayoral baby is still being talked about. Mr. Harold Smith, at the Navy meeting at the Town Hall on Wednesday evening, offered his hearty congratulations, and there was general applause. The Mayor, acknowledging, said that he would tell hisS daughter all about their kind wishes when she was old enough to understand —and she is a, precocious child.

Mr. George Mather will never sing 'Won't you buy my pretty flowers?" He always gives. them away. At the conclusion of the Board of Guardians' meeting on Wednesday be astopishe.d his fellow members by rising and ,walking across to Mrs. Hall and handing her two beautiful roses, remarking that he could do no other, as she was the only /lady Guardian present.

. Members of the Board of Guardians pa Wed- nesday thought it was mere courtesy which prompted the Town clerk to forward them the Council's resolution regarding free re-vahcina- tion of persons in the borough, but the netilth Committee's minutes reveal that the Local Government Board suggested its despatchl

Mr. Stewart .Royston, of Poddingt.m con- tinues to receive support for his work of provi.l- - walking ,sticks for wounded soldi .re. and also expressibns of gratitude from those who are benefiting from his thourzhtfi..l

The attendances nt the Warrington, lnsurrinc'e Committee during the last bompleted year were remarkably good on the ''s'vhole.

His Worship the Mayor, who is chairman, at- tended 36 times out of a possible 43, which, in view of his many engagements, was a fine record. Highest possibles were made by Mr. E. Burk, 19; Mr. P. CAMEOS.

By The Schoolmaster.

Up the Spout.

The right point of view and the right sense of proportion Are valuable assets. A hopeful nine-year-old came in school bearing a five- foot length of spout. "Please, sir, I've broken the spout," he said. "How" I asked. "I was climbing on the roof for a ball, and it came down,' he ex- plained., "But it didn't come down of itself," I urged. "No, sir," he explained further, "I was hanging. on to it, and I came down, too." He grieved over the spout, or in anticipation of punishment; I rejoiced over unfractured humanity. My thankfulness forbade punish- ment; perhaps he wondered why. Which point of view was correct? Perhaps some en- lightened educationist will guide me.

===== 0002601_19160729_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
Thoroughness.

The chosen people are a great race. Without monarch, government or country—they pros- per; "Moses and the profits "—'tis a happy phrase. The Jew is sober; he tolerates no paupers; he contributes largely to charities. In my class at Manchester I had several Jews; one, aged ten, was a really spicy, greasy specimen. Often had I sent him to wash; as often he got dirty again. One morning I sent him to the lavatory. "And wash yourself properly," I impressed; "properly, you understand." Ten, fifteen, twenty minutes passed, and Israel came not. I went to investigate. There, on a slopstone, in purls naturalibus (which means naked), stood my spicy little one, washing himself—properly, you under- stand. "Well," I said, "you've done your best; get dressed, sharp." But he wasn't any less greasy, even after that; so I gave him up, and he seemed content.

A Cure For Snoring. In the elementary school the teacher bears sway 271 hours out of 168 per week. The proper place to train teachers is the boarding school.

My teacher's cubicle was in a corner of the large dormitory; standing on my bed, I could command a view of the somnolent (sometimes!) community. Occasionally a lad snored; a habit strongly resented by the others. A little com- mon soap dropped in the mouth is an easy cure.

Once, in the stilly night, a snore of extra calibre resounded. "Throw your stockings at him," said a voice; and the stockings flew. These unavailing, other garments followed, but in vain; the horrid raucous rasp still prevailed. "I'll step him," said a gaunt, splay-footed lad. He went across the room; dipped his foot in the fire bucket; bopped to the offender's bed; threw down the bedclothes; lifted the delin- quent's nightshirt; and planted his flat, drip- ping foot plump on the snorer's warm abdomen. Shades of Blackpool! Jump from a cheerless bathing machine into cold water that just reaches your diaphragm. Then you will under- stand how the snorer's rasp suddenly change.l to a strangled " Ow! ow!" interlarded with dark threats.

The treatment was drastic, but effective. Ho never snored again.

Is Filial Solicitude Decadent?

Old fogies say, "Yes. Children have no con- sideration for their parents; eh! things were different when I was a lad—ditto—ditto—ad infinitum.".

Against this view I can produce important testimony. A parent thus writes to me:— "Dear Mr. Filbert,—Please excuse Jacky being absent as he as had the dierear to oblidge his mother."

True, ray own children were never so obliging as Jacky. Such considerate offspring may be few. Still, they ipso facto refute the argument of the fogies. The times are not decadent; filial devotion is yet te be fens+

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JULY 29, 1916.

SIR ARTHUR CROSFIELD AS POET.

Effort For The Blind.

Sir Arthur, Crosfleld has this week published his work "A Pilgrimage of Empire," through the London firm of Eyre and Spottiswode.. It ,is to be sold at 2s. 6d. and is. according to the binding, and the proceeds of the-sale will be devoted to Sir Arthur Pearson's fund ifor those who have lost, their eyesight in the war. As those, who have read the fairly long extracts from the poem, which we have already given in "The Examiner,". will know, it shows Sir Arthur in the character of a polished writer of verse, both descriptive and prophetic. • Yesterday's "Daily News" says that "A Pilgrimage of the Empire" "deserves a wel- come both on its own 'merits and for the sake of the cause to which the proceeds of the sale are to be devoted. The poem, opening with-a call to arms, takes the reader through the countries of the Empire to Egypt, where the Sphinx is represented as surveying the Empires which have come and gone in the course of the last five thousand years—the moral being the failure of brute force as a ruling factor. The concluding stanzas return to the united front Presented by , the British, Australian and Canadian soldiers and sailors to the common enemy." • Say the concluding lines :

Shoulder to shoulder through the shades of night, . - Together they have fought, but earliest dawn , Now glimmers in the East with fairy light. And heralds golden glory of the morn When peace on earth shall yet again be born. Then shall our warriors in their hosts return, With glory of achievement to adorn Their triumph, from all kith and kin to earn The gratitude and pride with which all hearts shall burn.

===== 0002601_19160729_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
GUILD OF HELP SECRETARY.

Rowland Retires After Seven

Years' Work.

Mr. Wilfrid J. Rowland having resigned the iieeretaryship of the Warrington Guild of flelp to take u-) an appointment under the National War Savings Committee in London, an "Examin'er" interviewer sought him at, the office for a short :talk on the eve of his departure for the Metropolis.' Mr. Rowland said that he came to the town nearly seven years ago to become the first secre- tary of the Guild. While the interview was proceeding a working man dropped in to express warm thanks for a &ervice that had .been ren- dered by the Guild. It was just the kind of concrete .case that to a journsaiist sums up:the usefulness of such a society, but .the Secretary pointed Out that no public reference could be made to the details of the case. These must' be treated confidentially. lihe Success of the Guild depended upon it. 'Of course," said Mr. Rowland, "it makes it the more difficult to present a case to the public to convince them that the Guild is serving its purpose. Reports of work done in a, town of this size must be made in general terms. Men who have been helped do not likettheir distress blazoned abroad.

• "One great ,thing the Guild has done," re- marked Mr. Rowland, "has been to check a vast amount of indiscriminate' charity. • One result of continual- refusal of help to .persons previously dependent upon charity has been to open the door to regular employment. There are people to-day in Warrington, who, s!x years ago, were sponging on society and are now earning their livelihood as decent citizens. Mistaken charity often undermines character, and the district committees which we have had at work have checked a great deal of it."

Increased Cost of Food.

"Is there less distress nowadays?" was a question which led Mr. Rowland to Make a very interesting statement. "Yes, decidedly," he answered. "Even before the war employment in Warrington was brisk • and, of course, since the war began the labour market has been depleted and there has been a real shortage of male labour, but some people are beginning to feel quite seriously the increase in the cost of food."

"Have you had many complaining?" "Quite a number have Made such repre- sentations, but, of course, it is not the business of charity to subsidise *ages: the real remedy is to secure higher wages. Occasionally, there- fore, we have made representations to employers regarding certain individuals and increases have been granted. But where grades of labour are concerned, it is the business of the trade unions to make representations as to the in- creased cost of living." "What about war work?"

"Well, the Guild works in close conjunction with the Parr Hall administration, mentioning cases wherever necessary. But some interesting cases are dealt with by the Guild. One 'con- cerns the will of a seaman who went down in a great naval fight. Another is a long contested claim for dependence allowance, which reaches the Appeals Committee in London. "Some firms which are making allow- ances to the wives of employes at the Front have found that the money is being wastefully spent in several cases," said Mr. Rowland, "and so, by request, the Guild has stepped in and has undertaken the spending of some of the money on necessary articles for the family, such as the purchasing of filothes, the supplying of milk and food. The money has been handed over to us and we have acted as almonei. I am happy to say, however, that in Warrington such cases have been the excep- tion."

During the time Mr. Rowland has acted as secretary it may be said that valuable work has been done in lifting up the standard of child life in the borough, for as a.direct outcome of the efforts of the Guild, two--Mothers' and Babies' Welcomes have been established in Catherine-street and Cairo-street, and thither the mothers take their young children weekly, receiving a cordial welcome and wholesome advice and assistance as to the manner in which their charges may be safely reared.

QUEEN *ALEXANDRA'S ROSE.

By R. Ossulston-Riche.

Won't you buy a dainty wild rose, Sir ? A'dainty wild rose; A flower that will not droop and die Like the bloom which giows. Allow me to fix one to your coat The wearing of it -shows— That you love your fellow-men, Sir, through Queen Alexandra's .Rose.

Won't you buy a pretty wild rose. Miss? A pretty wild rose; They're only imitation, Miss, which Ey'rybod v knows. But then they realise "some" money— For human kindness flows— When the people see the symbol of Queen Alexandra's Rose.

Some thirty million roses are. Out on sale to-day; Made, by cripples, for noble cause! What is that you say? "They've done their little bit." Yee, and yan? Well, you do yours, and pose As "Benevolence "—through the emblem of Queen Alexandra's Rose.

We have no rose days at present in this im- mediate neighbourhood, but the lines by Mr. R. Ossulston-Riche, our old actor-soldier-poet friend, which we produce to-day, might be • preserved for the occasion when next the flower sellers take possession of the streets. The lines exactly, meet the situation, being the appeal of a man who writes with his heart in his pen. A copy of the poem was sent to Queen Alexandra for Her Majesty's acceptance. '

PRIZE DAY.

69 Old Bo!eler Grammar School Boys Holding,COmmissions.

HONOURS IN SCHOLARSHIP AND WAR.

The Rev. Canon Willis (vice-president) distributed the ,prizes to students at the War- rington Grammar School on Thursday after- noon. He said he was much interested in the debate in the House of Lords on education, and specially in the speeches of Lord Croper and Lord- Crewe. The latter said that the German had still to -teach people to appre- ciate the characteristics of an English gentle- 'man, which stands for honesty and for courtesy. He would also like to add the characteristics of a Christian gentleman, piety and purity of life. That was a Christian school—a Church school. Lord Crewe said the distinction be- tween instruction and education was, instruc- tion was imparting knowledge and education was drawing out character; and he (the Rector) added that at the basis of character there must be religion. _ - -

The Rev. Horace Gray (headmaster) regretted that, under present circumstances, which they all deplored, he could not make Mention of any prominent University distinctions, but there were several gratifying honours. In January E. H. Littler gained a boy clerkship (30tt1 place in .United Kingdom and first in Liverpool district). J. A. Fox, walking in his brother's footsteps, gained the only entrance scholarship offered in open competi- tion into Stonyhurst College. Both these sons of Dr. Fox started in the preparatory class, and their success seemed to prove that the Governors were well advised in making this ,provision for the education of junior boys. F. J. Sobee passed the preliminary examination for the elementary school teachers' certificate. L. J. Witts, concluding a verY honourable school career, was recently awarded the Theodores Exhibition for modern languages at Manchester University. Thirteen boys passed the Oxford Local -Examinations, nine gaining honours, with six distinctions.

Men To Be Proud AN. Referring to the, part played by the "old boys" in the great conflict for righteousness which, the Empire is engaged in, the Head- master said so far as he had been able to ascertain, 169 old Botelerians had , responded to the call of King and country. (Applause.) No fewer than 69 of these are holding com- missions, and well-merited honours have been bestowed on them. Lieutenant-Colonel Brereton Fairclough, in command of a South Lancashire Battalion, has been twice mentioned in dispatches and received at His Majesty's' hands the C.M.G. Major W. B. Ritchie was awarded the D.S.O. and is now colonel in command of a King's Own Royal Lancaster Battalion. They were all delighted to learn of Major Gordon Roberts's promotion to the rank of Colonel in comMand of a battalion of the Lancashire Fusiliers. Other honours are those 'of Regimental Quartermaster-Ser- geant J. Whitehouse," who received the D.C.M., hnd Probationary Midshipman Reginald Allen, who gained the , D.5.C.,/ both ' for meritorious service in the Gallipoli cam- paign. (Applause.) Amidst deep and respectful silence the Headmaster then read out the ,names of "old boys" who had made the great sacrifiee, in the order in which they were admitted to that old foundation :

Fleet Paymaster John S. Place, who fell in the Jutland naval battle; Sergeant Ivan Fulton-Smith, of the Royal Scots; Captain G. W Owen, 10th Manchester Regiment, and Captain Charles T.' CADETS INSPECTED.

4:01. Meggridge Encourages Grammar

School Boys:

A youth and a fiery dog chased a .score 9f sheep off the pitch at the Arpley Cricket Ground on 'Tuesday afternoon to make- way for the Grammar School .Cadets, who were assembling for inspection •by Colonel Moggridge. Seventy-six lads were on parade, and Captain Pearson arranged them as a Service Company when on church parade—the tan ones at the ends and the gradually lessening heights of the others forming the platoon into .a picturesque festoon. .

When the Inspecting Colonel's taxi was heard humming along the' Cadets were caned to at- tention, and on his intimate approach a General Salute was offered and acknowledged. A considerable number of people were present to view the inspection, and afterwards watch the Cadets at work in sections. Prominent members of the Volunteer Training Corps viewed the, scene, eagerly as if after points. But most of those present were the relatives of the lads. The sun was strong, and the 'withers and sisters sought shelter under parasols. It gave a garden party tone to the proceedings. 4 particularly interested and interesting visitor was Captain Chambers, whose arm is still in a sling, due to the fighting in the great • advance. The best-known gentlemen present were the Headmaster (Rev. Horace Gray),. the

Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock), Aldermen Smethurst, Tinnion and Bennett, Councillor Henry Roberts, Mr. F. A. Frost, Mr. A. Bottomley, Mr. W. Webster and the Rev. J. Davies.

FOR OUR SAILORS.

Splendid Prospects For Success Of Navy Day.

MR. HAROLD SMITH'S VISIT.

Ladies and ,gentlemen of all political parties seated in the Council Chamber at the Town Hall, on Wednesday evening, raised their hands to signify their willingness to unite in making "Navy Day," on, Thursday, August 17th, a SUCCOSS.

This demonstration followed appeals by the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock) and Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., the former remarking that at the stalls in the Park on that day they were not going to have all Liberals at one, all Tories at another, and all Labour at another. "Mix up together," he added. "Let there be no party politics. This is a.people's war and we want to assist the Navy all together. Mix up with each other, so that at the end of the day you won't know what you are." The good humour of the Mayor was shared generally; also his strong determination to make **" The Day" worth celebrating as far as the Navy is concerned. Si I• Arthur Crosfield, Bart., Councillor Henry Roberts and Mr. W. Peter Rylands were among those who sent letters of apology for non- attendance.

Mr. ,Smith, who Was attired in his naval uniform., said that little argument was neces- sary to bring home to the people of Warring- ton the undying duty they owed to those on the sea. For some twenty months he had been in close contact with those in whose hands lay the whole fortunes of our Fleet—and also the whole fortunes of every one in this country. He lay no claim to being a sailor—he was only a very bad amateur—but he had speedily learnt two things: that the Navy never grumbled and never begged. Eicery penny of Admiral Jellicoe's fund for his wounded men or their widows and orphans had been given voluntarily. Most efforts seemed to be made on behalf of our gallant Army, and he felt that the Navy was being neglected. He, there- fore. appealed to Warrington to set an example to the whole of England by putting on record their debt to the brave men who,, by their vigilance, daring and •patience on the seas, were the bulwark and gurety of England. He did not think any other town had. done what the Mayor and himself were going to ask War- rington to do. The Navy deserved all they could offer, for by their protection of us from the foe, and by their care for our mercantile marine, they had kept us alive and would do so until the end of the war. (Applause.) The programme and names of all helpers will be announced later. Broadly speaking, there will be a fete in Bank Iltirk, with specially 'attractive features. Those concerned in votes of thanks to the Mayor and Mr. Smith for the part they are taking in the project were Alderman Bennett, Mr. Edward Allen, and Councillor W. H. J. Jenkins, who spoke "as one who had the honour and .pleasure of serving in His Majesty's Navy in my youthful days." As the meeting broke up, the ladies and gentlemen willing to help gave their names to Mrs. W. P. Rylands, who is acting as secre- tary. SIR ARTHUR CROSFIELD'S ABSENCE.

Sir Arthur Crosfield would have been present at the meeting, but arrangements had been made to give an afternoon at West Hill, High- gate, last Wednesday to the nurses of the hospital of which Lady Crosfield is command- ant. He and Lady Crosfield had, of ccnirse, to stay in London to fulfil that engagement.

STOCKTON HEATH ADULT SCHOOL

PICNIC.

On Thursday, the Women's Adult School, Stockton Heath, had a great outing, when over twenty members having taken electric car to Bank Quay, travelled by train to Frodsham, and thence strolled to Newton. The picnic. was arrange 4 by Mrs. :Sherburn, ,Whitefield-road, and although the walk to Newton-by-Frodsham was a rather long one, a lavish knife and fork tea soon put the party in the highest of good spirits. This repast was provided at Dr. Stephenson's National Children's Home and Orphanage. An inspection of this fine institution afforded special enjoyment—all the mothers amongst tha visitors being hugely interested. A number qf little girls were operating at the wash tub and there was also an efficient ironing section. Both working parties were highly commended. The verdict of experienced matrons on the nursery management was highly gratifying. It is a pity that our public institutions are not visited oftener by such observant juries. Having finished their inspection of the Orphanage our excursionists went up to Mersey View and *spent the remainder of the evening in a frivolous manner—joy rides on round- abouts and such like. They got back to Stock- ton Heath about 9.30.

Duty and Pleasure Hand-in-hand.

After inspecting the boys with considerable punctiliousbess the Ccdonel addressed them, remarking upon his pleasure at seeing the keen- ness with which they had tackled their work. It was obvious from what he had seep of their work and the rapidity with .which They had picked up so many things, that they had wanted to learn. On most occasions it was perhaps true to say that our duty and our pleasure w3ro very much separated. He hoped the boys wculd always do their duty when it- came to a choice of that kind. There were, however, happy. ex- ceptions when duty and pleasure went hand-in- hand, aid he thought their work in connection with, the Cadet Corps formed one of them. They could not have made such progress. and shown so much keenness unless they had de- rived pleasure from the work. They were much to be congratulated on all they had achieved under the command of Captain Pearson, who, he was sure, received every possible support in the work of the corps from the head master. " You are now preparing yourselves" added the Colonel, "to undertake a duty whenever occasion demands. There are, unfortunately, a few—l think we are all ashamed of them—who put their own feelings and foibles before their duty to the State. I think we can leave them alone, for I am sure none of you boys would ever do that. When you grow up and go out into the world I believe you will find that ,the dis- cipline you have undergone as .soldiers—young though you are—will not be wasted The country at large must be grateful to schools and schoolmasters who support this cadet corps movement. There are a great many people who think it ought to be compulsory in all schools to learn something of soldiering, and. lam bound to say agree with it. MY thanks are due to Mr. Gray, Captain Pearson and Mr. Pape for the trouble they have taken and also to you boys for having backed them up so well. I wish you good luck, and I dare say some of you will eventually join the Army.' You are affiliated to a very distinguished corps —the 4th South Lancashire Regiment—who have done very fine work at the Front, and you ought to be proud to be associated with them (Hear,' hear.) we hear occasionally from people at the Front of what is going on there, and I heard this morning from an old Warring_ ton Grammar Scliool BoYCOlonel Ritchie—who is now commanding a battalion of the Royal Lancaster Regiment. He and his brother at- tended .your .school as. boys. In his letter he tells me that the Lancashire boys are doing perfectly splendidly. They are setting before us all a fine example." • (Cheers.) Ringing cheers were then given the Colonel; and the company then. separated into sections to resume the work they had been so eloquently encouraged to pursue. . Yesterday 65 of the Cadets left the Central ! Station- at 11 o'clock for Chatsworth, -where they are to encamp with about 500 members of similar corps. As they moved out of the station they displayed • the . entlicsiasM of their compeers in the Regular Army'. .." THE GIRL AND TliE ‘.SEAL r—,st the 'Hippodrome.—{AdvW . : •

The New Allowance Scheme.

The question of allowances to families of men who have either had remunerative employment or ran small businesses with concurrent obliga- tions arose at the last local Military Tribunal. Alderman Tinnion remarked that, judging from the reports which had appeared in the Press, the manner in which the applications for grants were being dealt with was somewhat of a scandal. It was stated that the aderage grant was 6s. 20., and, as few were said to have received less than that sum; it was obvious that not many could have received more. He had also heard that in some cases in which the family income had hitherto been .t3' a week or more, no response to the 'application for grants had yet been received. If the Commissioners were distributing assistance on such meagre lines it :must make members of the Tribunal hesitate before giving decisions in cases in which financial hardship was pleaded.

Warrington and District Agricultural

Show.

Many valuable prizes, cups and medals are presented for competition at the 27th annual show of the Warrington and District Agricul- tural Society, which will be held in the Brewery Field, Wilderspool, on Thursday. This Society is a member of the Federation of Lancashire and Cheshire Agricultural Societies, and, granted favourable weather, which seems prob- able, the show should secure a largo amount of support. We are informed that there is a capital entry for pedigree Shires and dairy cattle. Judging in the latter classes will com- mence at 10 o'clock, and the pedigree Shires will be judged in the afternoon. Prize lists and other information may be obtained from the Secretary, Mr. James Bainbridge, Walton Estate Offibe. The entry of pedigree Shires far exceeds that of last year.

Darbyshire, J. Dodd. Free Churches' War Celebration Service.

In connection with the second anniversary of the war on Friday next, the, Free Chu rc n Council have arranged for a solemn united intercession service to be held in Bold-street Wesleyan Church at 7.30. The President of the Free Church Council, Rev. W. T. Derry, will pre- side, and the speaker will be the Rev. T. T. James (ex-president). The Rev. H. Ogden (secretary) will read the lessons. The Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock) has intimated his intention of being present, and it is expectnd that members of the Town Council will also attend. An invitation has been sent to Colonel Moggridge .and men under his command at, Orford Barracks. During the service the num- bers of Warrington Free Churchmen at the Front will be given, and the names of those members who have fallen during the war will be announced from the pulpit. After the ex- penses of the meeting have been defrayed the surplus collection will be devoted to war charities.
