£175 TO BE SPENT

On Converting Dutton Workhouse

Into Military Hospital.

The members of the Runcorn Board of Guardians met on Thursday to consider a scheme prepared by Messrs. Wright and if amlyn (public health surveyors) for the accommodation of wounded soldiers at Dutton Workhouse. I _ _

The Chairman. (Mr. C. E. Linaker, jun.) said that when the question was before the Guardians some time ago it was suggested that the rooms over the Board Room should be utilised as a hospital. The scheme, however, fell through because only 25 beds could be offered. Since then it had been found that with only a small expenditure the rooms pre- viously offered to the military authorities could be used as the Union hospital, which would enable them to offer the present hospital for ,the treatment of wounded soldiers. There would be room for seventy beds. Mr. Linaker, sen., moved that, subject to the cost not being prohibitive, the scheme be approved. It might be thought, he said, that they were spending the ratepayers' money, but our soldiers were fighting for the ratepayers. It was the duty of the Guardians to help them in their sufferings by providing proper hos- pitals. The Chairman said that the military authori- ties allowed 38. per patient per day. Mr. Linaker's resolution was carried. Mr. Hamlyn estimated that the total cost of putting the buildings into proper condition for. the reception of patients would be £175. The Chairman: We should have, to expend &VI of that for ordinary maintenaneo.. THE GIRL AND THE SEAL "—at the Efippodromo„,—(A4vt.l

Tanning Labour Shortage. Tanners have been notified that ' all the. leather they can produce suitable for army requirements will be wanted practically up to the end of the year. They will loyally strive to meet requirements, but 'within the next few: weeks they have the depletion of labour to con- tend with, as with the taking of single men up to 30 years of age and married men Up to 25, it will be taking the cream of the men out of most of the works. The bulk of these men are generally on the heaviest work, and it will be hard toreplaeethem.—Warrington correspondent of the "Leather Trades Review."

"THE DIAMOND FROM THE SKY."

£2,000 Prize Story In Pictures.

A great serial prize story is being shown at three Warrington picture houses—The Grand, Bridge Foot; The Pavilion, Sankey Green; and the Central, Sankey- street. "The Diamond from the Sky" won ,for its author a prize of £2,000. It is full of dramatic interest, the incidents being sensational and masterful to a degree. As an aid to full appreciation of the pic- tures, "The Examiner" is publishing the story in full week by week. Every Satur- day we publish the instalment of the re- markable plot that is revealed by the pictures in the following week at the two first named houses. We also publish every week a summary of the story from the beginning to date, so that any patron of any of the houses, may know by reading "The Examiner" exactly the history of the pictures he is going to see. " Examiner " readers who patronise the houses mentioned should make this im- portant fact known to their friends.

Child Neglect.

The National Society for the Prevention of . Cruelty to Children investigated 3,824 com- plaints of neglect and cruelty in England, Wales and Ireland during the month of June. Of the 3,745 completed cases, 3,632 were found true, affecting the welfare of 11,166 chile.ren, and involving 4,564 offenders. Warnings were issued in 3,303 cases; 164 were prosecuted (re- sulting in 163 convictions), and 165 were dealt with by transfer or in other ways. From its foundation in 1884 the society has dealt with 916,615 complaints, involving 2,569,434 children. In the Warrington Branch during the same month 11 cases were dealt with, affecting 35 children.

Belgian Gratitude. The Belgians who until recently were the guests of the Working Men's Mission in War- . rington, but are now in France, have written the following letter, which appears in this month's Mission Magazine :—" Just a feW words to let you know we arrived safe at Le Havre. Not necessary to say how happy the meeting was with Mons. and Mde. Plaquee, who werel waiting anxious by the boat. We were all very tired and a little bit poorly frort the long travelling. , We think you shall be , very glad now ,you know we are safe in France. We are very far from you all, but we are always talking and thinking about Warrington And it 6 kind people. We have been very comfortable • in your town. However can we thank yo. enough for all what you have clone for tor Please give our best and true thanks to the Mission people, and tell them we can never forget them. Well, it is 11,1', possible to show you all our kindness. We el; send you our greetings, and hope to see you ell again some day."

PRAISE FOR WARRINGTON

AND TE:RRITORIALS.

Colonel George Crosfield, D. 5.0., after an absence from Warrington extending' over nearly two years, returned to his home, Tower House, Lodge-lane, on -Thursday afternoon. It was a quiet and. unostentatious home- °ming, altogether characteristic of a brave man and genuine patriot. Warrington had promised to give itself the pleasure of according to Colonel Crosfield a reception worthz of a gentleman who left them as a Territorial Major and had done such deeds for his country dur- ing the greatest crisis in her history that he had come back to them with the added rank of Colonel, with the command or a Welsh Regi- Ment, and possessing the coveted Distinguished Service Order for having shown conspicuous ability in .the field. Twice or thrice has Colonvl erosfield been mentioned in dispatches. These are some of the outward signs of a .claim he has upon the respect and 'admiration of his .fellow townsmen and, substantial though th.-? 3, may be, they are outweighed by the claim he has upon the personal affection of us all. A born soldier who knew how to direct, and a born gentleman who knew how to win the hearts of those he had to direct, Colonel Cros- field, the men told their friends and relations in cauntless letters from the thick of the fight, ,made his men ready to go wherever he miglit lead. When the gallant Colonel was wounded in the leg a few Months ago there was not a home in Warrington where the sorrow of the occasion was not felt most acutely as a personal grief; it was indeed recognised that another debt of love had been contracted that would have to be honoured in the future. , But there was no demonstration on Thursday. Colonel Crosfield, who had been staying suc- cessively with Sir Arthur and Lady Crosfield in London, Mrs. John Crosfield in Buckingham- shire, and Miss Truda Crosfield at Deganwy, reached Warrington by train from the last named Welsh resort about 4 o'clock. No one knew of his arrival, but many were the respect- ful salutes from all classes of the people who recognised his familiar features as he passed them in his car.

COLONEL CROSFIELD AND HIS INJURIES. A representative of " The Examiner" accounted himself extremely fortunate to be received by the Colonel within a couple of hours 0f his arrival home. It was the result of a three weeks' vigil—a professional, journalistic 100k-out kept up in order that the worthy Colonel should not altogether succeed in coming back among us as though nothing had happened sincethe departed. Our representative found Colonel Crosfield sitting in his billiard room looking the picture Of health and surprisingly buoyent in spirit. On being congratulated upon looking so ,well, Colonel Crosfield said he never felt better, senied to think 'very little about the injury to left leg, and expressed the intention of being "back again" before very long. 'But not. on active service?;" suggested cur re p r ese ntat ive.

"Oh, yes,':" he declared. "My leg, as you see, was amputated very low down. I have he complete use of my knee joint, and -.•,hen tile time comes for the fixing of an, artificial foot you will scarcely know the difference." But it was not of himself the Colonel Iv:, Prepared to speak. He ,was, he explained, tem- Porary Colonel of a Suffolk Battalion for ,six Weeks, during the absence of. their Colonel; and then he was given the command of the Royal 'Welsh. It was soon after he was given that command that he was wounded, and it Was in this connection that he made the start- ling, if pleasantly reassuring statement, that he expects' to be eventually passeti for service at the Frcn L.

"Have you any message for Warrington?" "Only that I think Warrington has done nPlendidly in the way of enlistment. I should also like to say that the Warrington Terri- torials did jolly gooa work out at the Front. and Warrington has every reason to be proud of them."

THE VALUE OF MILITARY TRAINING.. "I suppose .the experiences of the war hare confirmed your .ppinion .as ho the need for National Service and made the public more inclined to the idea than they were?"' 1' I think," said Colonel Crosfield, "that 'I 'would rather not enter at the present juncture uPon what was a hikhly controversial subject; talt—if you ask me point-blank—l think people 'lave swung round on the question of National Service. I have had many talks with different DeoPle, who agree that the physical benefits conferred upon our young men by the military training they have had have been enormous. If only for that reason I am sure the great mass of the people feel that it would be an awful Ilaistake if we did not continue some sort of tational training after the war." 'And about keeping up our military Strength r' "However great may be our victory oier Germany" he replied—"the world being what it is---it would be folly to assume that there is ;lever' going to be war again. Therefore, it will °a- just as incumbent upon to as ever it was to aea that we are properly prepared:" 'The Territorials have done very good work. cenpying a kind of half-way house between National Service and leaving the Regular Forces Unstrpported altogether?" 'Yes," said Colonel Crosfield.- "I should to put it on record that the Territorials have rile an exceedingly useful work—but they had (0 be kept at home for six months' training. Of 'ourse, while so training they were doing a important duty on home defence." , " Sir Arthur and Lady Crosfield are coming Itere to-morrow to stay with-me for the week- !rid," was Colonel Crosfield's closing remark, ,une homely finish to a cheery interview granted lbY one who has laboured and suffered for his Country's welfare, and persists in treating as nothing his personal injury in view of what we "11 know he has materially helped to achieve as the result of his labours. „Colonel Crosfield then left for the Warrington and was again freely recognised by many no were in the thoroughfares on the way to tio_ld-street.

Sitting beside him in his car for part of the cgirnev, our representative asked Colonel rosfield whether he experienced the usual feel- of gladness at being home again after the ILDss of nearly, two years, Yes," he replied, "I am glad to see the old 'wit once again, but I am anxious,to get back."

CORPORAL H. DAVIS.

Blinded For Three Days By Gas Fumes.

Wounded in October, Corporal Harry Davis, of the Royal Engineers, only son of Mr. and Mrs. T. F. Davis, 67, Longford-street, has now been gassed and removed to hospital at Oxford. Writing from there under date iuly 19th, he says:—

'7`l have got a little dose of gas. It occurred last Friday morning at 1 a.m. I have had the bandage taken off my eyes, which were affected most. I could not see for three days, but can see all right now, although my eyes seem a bit queer, now and again. This hospital is a splen- did place and I am getting on all right 41 it." Corporal Davis, who is 22 'years of age, joinod the A.S.C. in 1912, but about twelves months ago he transferred to the Royal Engineers. He was formerly employed in the laboratory at Nlessrs. Jos. Crosfield and Sons' Works. He received his earlier education at the Hamilton- street School, and later went to the Secondary `',?hool, where he remained for three years.

GUNNER A. ASTALL.

Killed Along With Comrade By

Chance Shell.

Mr. and Mrs. Astall, 10, Erwood-street, have received official news that their son, Gunner A. Astall, of the Royal Field Artillery, was -killed in action on July Sth. In addition to the official irtimation, the followin,-; letter has been received from a Lieutenant:--" I am extremely sorry to have to inform you that your son was killed in action on Saturday, the Bth inst. He and a N.C.O. were sent out to look for, a suit- able spot to establish a signalling station, and

4 A

while doing so the Germans started shelling R wood through which they were passing. As far as I can gather they took cover in a dug-out and a chance shell struck it, killing them" both. Your son had not been very long in this battery, but during the short time he was here, he did, very well. He was buried in a soldiers' ceme- tery not far from where he fell. Please. accept my sincere sympathy in your loss. There is some consolation the fact that your son ,has 'done his duty well.' "

• Gunner Asiall, who was 21 years of age, enlisted in March, 1915, after having been rejected three times in 1914, and went to France in January of this year. Ile wai formerly employed at Messrs. Rylands Bros. Ltd., and was educated at Silver-street School.

D!ED OF WOUNDS.

RIFLEMAN D. WALSH.

"Was Prepared And Resigned To Die," Says Chaplain.

A week last Monday Mr. and Mrs. P. Walsh, of 34, Old-road, received news that their son, Rifleman Daniel Walsh, of the Rifle Brigade,

RIFLEMAN DANIEL WALSH. Died of Wounds.

had been dangerously wounded, and on Friday of last week they were officially informed that he had died of his wounds. • In addition to the intimation from the War Office, the following letter, dated July 16th, was received from a Chaplain:—" It will be con- soling to you to know that your boy, D. Walsh, received the sacraments while fully conscious, and that he was prepared and resigned to die. He. died at 2.30 p.m. yesterday. His, chaplain, Father Ingram, was with him when he was wounded on the night of the 13th, and I was with him the following morning and gave him Holy Communion and visited him several times afterwards. I was also with him when he died. He received every care and attention. This afternoon I blessed his grave at Reny Station. A cross, with his name, 'Sze., inscribed thereon, marks the spot. He received all the rites of the Church, and I am sure this knowledge Niri 11 help ~to comfort you in your affliction." Rifleman Walsh, who was 21 years of age, enlisted a fdrtnight after the outbreak of war, and had been in France twelve months. Be- fore enlisting Ire-- was employed as a moulder by Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Co.. Ltd. lie was educated at Our Lady's R.C. School, Latchford, and attended the same church. He was a member of the Catholic Young Men's billiard team. His brother-in-law. Private Jack INoran, served at the Front for fifteen months with th. South Lanes. Regiment, and was afterwards discharged as a time-expired man.

"A few days later the Moniteur wrote: Bona- parte is comjng upon. us rapidly.' Nothing Heard Of Him Since July Ist.

PRIVATE J. A. ALLEN Wounded.

convoy was passing through to Winwick Hos- pital. Although averse to looking at bandaged men, she conquered her feelings on this occasion. All at once a motor car passed by, one of whose occupants was waving a demon- strative greeting. The wireless telegraphy of a mother's love immediately responded: "Why, there's James!" she exclaimed in surprised and anxious tones. Her daughter pooh-poohed the idea—they had just been telling a 'friend that the young man was in France. "Yes, •it's James," replied Mrs. _Allen. decisively._ _

_ It was James—a Private in the R.A.M.C. He had waved his right arm—the other one Had had a dose of shrapnel which made a big wound below the elbow. Mrs. Allen soon got con- firmatory news from Winwick, Hospital,. where her son is now receiving the best of care and attention. 'The wound is a nasty one, but sur- gical skill will almost surely prove triumphant. Private Allen .received his wound dtring' the operations at Trones Wood a week ago last Thursday. With three other bearers he was in • the act of placing a stretcher case in-- an ambulance when a great shell burst behind them. All the party were. wounded by ,portions of the exploded charge, the poor ,fellow on the stretcher being struck on the head. This shell was' deadly in its, accuracy. Four more R.A.M.C. men were carrying another wounded man. He was killed outright by the shock. Private Allen does not know what happened to his comrades. . Private Allen is 25 years of age, and joined the Army on the 4th November, 1915. He was drafted to France on the 15th May following. Born in Stockton Heath, he attended St. Thomas' Parish School, and was "finished off" at the People's College,- Warrington. Prior to the war he , was employed by Messrs. Henry Milling and Co., Ltd., in Tforsemarket-street. His father, Mr. Ebenezer Allen, is at. Present working in Essex. ,

SERGEANT H. CLIFFE.

"Our ' Kids ' Knocked Prussian Guards Over Like Skittles."

Sergeant Herbert Cliffe, of the Royal Scots, who, before enlisting, lived with his mother at 9, Robson-street, has been wounded in the big advance. Writing home, he says : ." I suppose you have followed the news •of the push.' .Nothing could stop . us. Our battalion did good •vork. We -4 elr. supports for the —th Brigade, and when they got stuck at the first line of German trenches we had orders to assist. . We cleared them out, and ' deuced ' smart. No mercy was given, only when we had to take prisoners. We took prisoners 200 officers and men. In one dug-out, forty feet deep; we spotted about four Huh officers and about twelve men.. The 6fficers were smoking cigars, and had revolvers in their hands just ready to ' pot 'us off. But there was `.nothing doing.' 'lfcw many down there, Mac?' About twenty.' 'ill right, four bombs sharp and let them divide- them. Get them in H to wait for the Kaiser.' We rushed 'straight through, and no swank. I got hit with a nose cap the next morning (Sun- day): It is nothing serious; only a few ribs broken, &c: No open wounds. My equipment and ammunition pouches saved me from going to 'Widnes.' Our losses were nothing com- pared to theirs. I couldn't help but laugh to see our ' kids ' knocking pig Prussian Guards over like skittles. They were demented and shaking like leaves .through our artillery fire. You would 'have given. ' quids ' to see our lads

taking helmets, revolvers, glasses, &c., from the Runs."

Sergeant Cliffe, who is 36 years of age, is brother to Mrs. Taylor, 72, Willis-street. He has served in the Army for about, 18 years. . was formerly employed at the Orford Tannery, and was educated at the National School.

PRISONERS-OF-WAR.

Continued Good Work At The Parr Hail.

Every week, in a spacious room in the Parr Hall, scores of neatly arranged and carefully assorted heaps of food-stuffs are displayed on long tables—one would think that a number of guests with tremendous appetites were expected to dinner. Each pile of comestibles is, how- ever, destined for. an eagerly expectant South Lancashire lad in some German camp where hunger is rather the rule than the exception. The, consignment this week gives an idea of what each wan in turn receives, and the list of articles is constantly changed, so as to afford variety in the diet. 211). loaf (well-baked and appetising). Condensed milk.

Tea and sugar. Lunch tongue. Jam. Cocoa. 2oz. tobacco and 5 packets cigarettes.

With each parcel is enclosed a tin of a pure vegetable preparation known as "Marmite," which is intended to counteract the effects of the constant use of tinned foods tol which our men are forced to resort.

It is most gratifying to see, as our reportez did the other day., the piles of acknowledgments from our men in the camps and the gratitude they express.

===== 0002601_19160729_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
BARE-FOOTED AT THE FRONT.

Newspaper Lad Is Wounded

And Then Runs Race.

Sitting on the counter at the " Echo " Office in Market-street, Warrington, yesteirday after- noon, and drumming his thickly booted heels against the structure was a khaki-clad youth who has had some great experiences at the Front, but who was now inquiring after his late ha'penny paper comrades: The youth with the benevolent face and in- quisitive tongue was Harry Mahoney, ,whose parents reside at 11, 'Collin-street. Not eightep until November, he celebrated last Christmas in the trenches, and was wanting to know what had happened to those he had left behind when he 'listed. He learned that two or three score have joined the Army as well as he, and that several chums will return no more.

Before enlisting Mahoney used to take the Latchford circuit, nearly always in his bare feet. Like other 'cute lads, he used to put his boots under the counter and, when his voci- ferous clammering had got a handful of papers, he leapt away vigorously and beeame a swift- footed figure, first stop Latchford: ; The reason Mahoney is now in Warrington is because of his bare feet habit. When attack- ing at Vimy Ridge several weeks ago he was slightly wounded in the left foot, but after a few days' rest was able to trot off the sick list. Soon afterwards a Brigade nine mile foot race was arranged, and Mahoney, remembering his lightning Latchford runs, gave his name as a competitor. . Re decided to take to the track bare footed. During the run his wound re- opened, dirt wormed i.ts way in, and blood poisoning ensued. For days he suffered horribly and then was able to be removed to an English hospital. happily, he is now well I and spending a few days in his native town. He is again using his feet—sitting on the Echo" Office counter and kicking his heels With old-time regularity.

===== 0002601_19160729_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
THE TEST OF BATTLE.

We are not good at shouting. in the street, At waving flags or tossing caps in air; We take our triumphs as we take defeat With scarce a hint of having turned a hair; And so our pride to-day Declines to boom itself the German way.

Yet we are proud. because at' last, at last • We' look upon the dawn of our desire; Because the weary waiting-time is passed And we have tried our temper in the fire; And, proving word by deed, Have kept the faith we pledged to France at need

But most because, from mine and desk and mart, Springing to face a task undreamed before, Our men, inspired to play. their prentice part Like soldiers lessened in the school of war, ' True to their breed and name W6nt flarA-Nss through the fierce baptismal flame.

Ana he who brOUght.these armies into life, ,And on them set the impress.of.his Could he he mov'ecl"by sound of..mortal strife" There where he lies, their Captain, cold'and still Under the shrouding tide, How would his giyat hart ,stir. and glow with pride! ,

--0.5., "Punch."

===== 0002601_19160729_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
"GOING SICK."

How The Military Surgeons Care For "Tommy."

THE PARADOX.

By SYDNEY JEFFERY.

At eight-thirty in the morning a small crowd' has collected outside the Orderly Corporal's bunk, the members of which are there for the purpose of "going sick" and "getting their forms filled in." " Tommy "is no Titan; he does have his ailments. Hardly, hcr.vever, as a direct result of Army life; for if he contracts severe indigestion it is, you may depend, on account of those three suppers he partook last night; or should he awake with a thorough "chill," he may blame only' himself for having vacated his bed to pillow-fight in the national costume of the Fiji Islanders. Whatever the cause, however, these ailments come; and are reported after breakfast. And the patient Orderly Corporal fills in the particulars pre- liminary to the sick report—name, age, religion, length of service, and so on. An hour later the party reassembles in the corridor, in charge of the senior "sick man"'; and marches across the town to the Garrison Auxiliary Hospital.. The hospital is crowded with sick men. In- deed, one would hardly believe sO many could "go sick!' in one day. Such being the case, however, it is necessary for each man to fall into the long queue outside the particular de- partment to which he has been directed, and await his turn. Should any man have brought' along a morning paper he then immediately finds himself a popular idol, whose claim to greittn'ess is that proverbial " page " which he can "rip off" and " iend " at his comrade's request. Yet amid so many one small news- sheet goes not far: and the majority, being left with zio better occupation than a survey of their surroundings, forthwith adopt it. Perhaps no- body 'could do so better. Have you ever notieed the stare of the British "Tommy "—that fascinating, compelling gaze accompanied by silence and immobility; which, he in- variably assumes when among unfamiliar sur- roundings or associates? It is a fearful and wonderful thing; the wonder-held mood of him whom one might imagine beyond ,all power of Wonder, remembering some of the sights it has been his to see. Could the uninitiated civilian pass through these corridors he might well be reminded of Henley's hospital verses—the thick air, the subdued shuffling, the occasional restless cough, the faint smell of drugs, the rapid pass- ing to and fro of officials, the infinite variety of " types " waiting with their thoughts. Not for long would there fade from his mind those hundreds of khaki-clad figures, sitting, standing, leaning, lounging—but all gazing; with thoughts to him impossible of conjecture.

===== 0002601_19160729_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
" TOMMY'S " THOUGHTS.

But "Tommy," as all hia comrades know, is thinking of a very simple thing. Not for him the depressing cares and fretting anxieties of the workaday civilian world. He is a mere child, and the State does everything for him. Little there is to worry him. • He is simply telling himself over and over again. with unique enthusiasna„that such hospitals are beastly dull holes henceforward to be 'avoided at any hazard! And true it is that the building is no gem of architecture. Though large and comprising so many perfectly-equipped depart- ments, 'it is but a plain erection of wood and zinc covered with very fresh paint: the corridors lined with plain forms and sprinkled by R.A.M.C. Corporals and Sergeants, whose busi- ness it is to collect and arrange the "sick forms" as the patients arrive. ' It is a tem- porary erection in time of war; and everything about it, from tile curt departmental directions to the very brisk surgeons, is tensely business- like.

At length, after an interval of some couple of hours, Private XYZ" is smartly wakened from his apathy by the calling of his name, and step- ping from the queue, is quickly ushered into the presence of :tho conimissidned surgeons; of whom the first to have completed the diagnosis he has on hand beckons him to step forward for examination. Graphically then, Private XYZ describes his symptoms; to be packed off, should his case be serious,. to one of the spqtless wards —pending further attention. He will be care- fully tended and again examined, perhaps by two or more surgeons. His case will be well discussed, and his position explained to him every tittle as efficiently as in civil life. He will have the opportunity of going before the gedical Board. Should the cure be a matter even of months, the military authori- ties will unhesitatingly sanction his retention in the Forces—one has spent seven months in hospital—and they certainly will mit discharge him as "unfit," nor deny him service, if he can reasonably be cured. Of course, attention so widespread, together with the operations and length of time involved, cannot but prove of enormous expense to tVe State; but the public knows that in the long run itself reaps interest a hundredfold! The cheerful acquiescence with stupendous taxation of "the great 8.P." has become sufficiently proverbial to render ; dilation upon this unnecessary. I And if "Tommy's" ailment is but slight, there is jotted down upon a slip of paper and handed to him a- prescription (unintelligible), which he at once conveys to the adjacent dis- pensary to receive his medicine—in a bottle for which he is charged one-ninth of his day's pay, if he has smashed the last issued him (as usually he has). Finally, he returns to camp or barracks, to be followed by the " report " and to resume or be excused duty according to the doctor's direction. And that is tall there is in "going sick."

===== 0002601_19160729_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
IS IT ALL?

But IS it all? Behind this trifling routine and drab monotony there reposes an epoch in the history of mankind. War, carried through the ages to its logical conclusion, has culminated in a paradox, and ridiculed itself. For the chiefest destruction of life the highest conserva- tion of life is vital. The fate of the 'universe, in. its Titanic struggle—that culmination of count- less ages of physical combat—rests not primarily with the warrior but with the surgeon and the scientist—maybe with the fusty old professor of failing vision and declining days. He is the breaker; the warrior is in comparison a straw upon the water. Brawh has been driven back upon brain, but not the " brain " we knew before; not cunningl treachery, plot, but that learning than which the material world can yield no higher. And when man has become satiated with the bestial instincts, he will dis- cover that the Divinity which shapes our ends. rough hew them how we will, has utilised these same instincts in stimulating—shall we say goading?—those finer arts to standards not known before.

From America.

Durifg a thunderstorm in the town of Berne, says the " Albanjr Knickerbocker," the electric fluid struck an apple tree against which a young man Lawrence was leaning. It split the tree from top to bottom," making a gap sufficiently large to let Mr. Lawrence slip in about a foot, immediately after which it sprung to and held him as tight as if he had been in a steel trap. Before he could be extricated a resort to axes and crowbars became necessary.

The New "Mare."

The "Worcester Chronicle" gives the follow- ing as a verbatim copy of a letter from a chief magistrate of a certain corporation :—" Dear Sur,—On Monday next I am to be made a Mare, and should be much obliged to you if so be as you will send me down by the Coatch some provisions fetting for the occasion, and am to ax my brother, the old Mare, and the rest of the Bentch. I am, sur, &c." The above ,was answered by a wag, into whose hands it fell, as follows :—" Sir,—ln obedience to your orders, I-have sent per coach two bushels of the best oats; and as you,are to treat the old Mare, have added some bran to make a maeh."

Stockton Heath Conscientious

Objector's Unearthly Views.

At a meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Tribunal the appeal of a Stockton Heath fore- man joiner was adjourned to enable him to bring evidence of his conscientious objection to military service. At Thursday's sitting the applicant stated that the whole tenor of New Testament Scrip- tures 'gas opposed to militarism. He based his case entirely on our Lord's teaching. He had had an objection to military service for 25 years, and claimed to be a minister Of the International Bible Students' Association. To, go against his convictions would bp to displease God.

A letter was read from Mr. Alfred Taylor, of Marsh House-lane, Warrington, stating that he knew of the appellant's 'objection to military service. The appellant was quite unconcerned about the material things of this.,,world, not caring to register his vote at Parliamentary or local elections, nor to take an interest in passing events. The appellant was to his .(Mr. Taylor's) mind, dead to the world. The Chairman: I'm very sorry to hear it. The Clerk asked whether the appellant would show his willingness to make i sacrifice for the sake of his conscience by taking work at £1 a reek, Appellatt replied that he could not live on gl a week; he had a wife and five children. He would not like to take up any work under the military authorities. The appeal was disallowed, the appellant being passed for combatant service. The Chairman: We are not convinced of your conscientious objection. ,

===== 0002601_19160729_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
To Get The Young Men Out.

In a case in which a Strettt n tanant farmer and hawker applied for exemption, Colonel Pilkington (the military representative) re- marked that as the maii had six children the cost to the country of sending him into the Army would be 295. a week. He had received instructions that morning to see that the old.cr men were got into munition works so as to release the younger men. ,

- The case was adjourned for a month, and appellant was told in the meantime to get employment in a munition works.

Disallowed.

The Tribunal disallowed the appeal of a Stockton Heath hairdresser. He had been in business nearly seven years. A similar decision was given in the appeal of a Stockton Heath master butcher and slaughter- man.

===== 0002601_19160729_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
Councillor Henri' Roberts's Difficulties

Councillor Henry Roberts, Warrington, asked for exemption for his gardener, basing his appeal upon the military services of his family, his own public services and his inability to obtain another gardener. He did not know how he could manage without this man. The war was pressing very unequally on difierent families. His family had not shirked a single responsibility, and had been carrying the burden for nearly two years. In addition to his voluntary work in connection with the war, he held many public appointments which were of national importance. Exemption was granted until the Ist Septem- ber, and the Tribunal reco,imended that the man should be medically examined.

===== 0002601_19160729_art0134.txt | included=true | included =====
Stockton Heath Businesses,

The Tribunal exempted a Stockton Heath fruiterer and greengrocer, whb claimed to be the sole owner of the business, until Ist Septem- ber, and a Stockton Heath fish and game dealer until the Ist October.

===== 0002601_19160729_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
Jobbing Gardener Gets Exemption.

George Harry Tandy, Ackers-lane, Latchford Without, applied for conditional exemption. Mr. Browne, for the applicant, pointed out that Mr. Tandy was a jobbing gardener aged 39, with four young children, and he not only had the management of his own business with six men now—there were ten before the war—but ha also had to manage the business of a nurseryman and florist of his father and mother' at The Ackers, Latchford Without. His father was paralysed and his mother unable to get out of ale house through chronic rheumatism. He had two brothers in France, both of whom were married, and he was the principal support of his parents.

The Tribunal granted conditional exemption, applicant to join the V.T.C.

-STRENGTH - AND HOW TO GAIN IT.

INSTEAD OF EXERCISES, PATENT FOODS AND MEDICINES, TAKE PHOSPHATE WITH YOUR MEALS.

The demand to-day is for men and women who are strong in every sense of the word—possessing the physical strength necessary to .madure hard- ships and fatigue; the mental strength to grapple with difficult problems; the nervous force which endows the body with vigour and vitality; the will power to triumph over adver- sity and, turn defeat into Victory. But such glorious streagth is impossible so long as your nerves are weak and exhausted, and therefore if you would'be really strong you must first care for your nerves. Weak, exhausted nerves need food, and it has been proved in numberless cases that the only food they can or Will absorb readily and naturally is' pure - —a well-known ,form of phosphate which most chemists stock in 5-gr. compressed tablets; so that if you feel your strength is failing from any cause, you should- get a supply of these bitro-phosphate tablets and take one with every meal. Practcally- all of the minor ailments afflicting mankind, as welYas many of the more serious maladies,, can be traced to nervous exhaustion and lowered vitality, and probably this explains why such a remarkable improve- ment in the general health is invariably notice-- able when bitro-phosphatc is taken as directed, and the nerves are thereby revitalised and made strong. Readers will be glad to learn that bitro-phosphate can now be obtained locally from H. M. Ashton, of .63, Sankey-street, as well as from most other high-class chemists everywhere.

LAYMYI

LOCAL FACTS AND FANCIES.

By CRITIC,

This beautiful weather, with which we have lately been favoured, seems to have been in- strumental in bringing quite a large number of visitors to the village.

Quite a large number of Manchester people have spent their annual vacation fishing in the lake.

O young sportsman, fishing from the public path told me that it was there that the best bf: catches were got. 'Nuff said!

The employes from the Lancashire Tool Works looked quite a merry party when they arrived back from Chester on Saturday evening.

Who was ,the "young blood" that struck the match in the railway tunnel to see where he was?

Did he think we were going to be treated to a severe storm? Or was he .endeavouring to light the gas?

An appeal is now being made throughout the village for feminine labour to assist the far- mers. Will all farmers needing labour kindly note?

The familiar " G.R. " brassard of the Volun- teers has now been discarded.

As every man takes the oath .he Vecomes a member of His Majesty's Forces.

And is entitled to wear his uniform without being advertised.

Parading at Heaton Park 'last Saturday, they created quite a good impression on "Tommy," who 'received them with open, arms.

Even the "fairer sex" were inclined to be nice.

Our worthy Councillors are to meet again next Monday. What will be the results? Wait and see.

During last week-end a somewhat alarming fire broke out at the Great Oak Farm, High Legh. •

Although the fire was soon extinguished, am informed that a considerable amount of damage was done.

Considerable excitement was created on Wednesday afternoon by the appearance of an aeroplane, which gracefully flew over the village at quite a low altitude.

Quite a large number of married then have been called to the Colours this week.

Primitive Methodist Sunday School Scholars' Treat —By kind permission of Mr. John Houghton, West Leh, the scholars and friends of the Primitive Mkhodist Sunday School were able to hold their- annual treat in the field adjoining his house, near lNlaltman's-road. Games and sports were provided for the scholars, and were conducted by Messrs. G. L. Welford, C. Wilkinson and H. Cole. Tea was provided in the Sunday school by Mrs. Wel- ford, and was daintily served to about 60 scholars and friends by Mesdames E. Moss, Sharples, H. Tongue and A. Struthers, and the Misses E. Tongue and L. Appleton. After tea the party were conducted round the magnificent gardens of West Legh by the genial head gardener, Mr. Broome, and as the shades of night drew nigh the party dispersed for their respective homes, well pleased with their afternoon's out- ing. To-day the senior scholars and friends are to attend a garden party at Dunham Massey. Congregational Sunday School Anniversary.— The anniversary services of the Congregational Sunday school were held in the chapel last Sunday, and were attended by large congrega- tions. The preacher for the day was the Rev. E. C. Scurrah, of Thirsk, father-in-law of tile present minister, the Rev. S. Gill. In the morn- ing he spoke on the child life of the future, and gtrongly advocated careful training of the men and women of the ,future. The afternoon ser- vice was devoted to the young people, and was attended by the scholars from the surrounding Nonconformist Sunday schools. Dr. Scurtak gave a very practical address, and the children sang special hymns. Miss M. A. Appleton sang with great taste "Come unto Me," and. Mies Lillian .Ward rendered in a charming manner "there is a happy land." A special feature of the afternoon's service was a collection of flowers contributed by the children, which were received by Mrs. Geo. Within, and plaf:ed the cent;e of the church. These flowers were afterwards sent to the Brookfield Military H9S- pital, and.. were much appreciated by the wounded "Tommies." In the evening special hymns and solos were again *rendered. Miss F. Smith, of Hale, sang with great feeling '‘` Lead, kindly light," and Mrs. C. Lewis sang "['he widow's mite," the chorus of which was greatly enjoyed by the large congregation. Throughout the day Mr. Albert Leather, the church organ- ist,- played special music, and in the afternoon a special prayer was rendered for the relatives of the late Pte. Thomas Read.

===== 0002601_19160729_art0139.txt | included=true | included =====
VOLUNTEERS AT HEATON PARK.

Demonstration Of Trenches.

Tile Altrincham Battalion of the Cheshire Volunteer Regiment, to which the Iymm and Ouihtrington Platoons belong, held a demon- stration of the trenches at Heaton Park last Saturday. The Lymm and Oughtrington Platoons, under the command of Captain G. R. Williamson, with Sergeants S. D. Moston, J. G. L. Farrar and T. Peters, fell in at Oxford- road Station and marched to Albert-squa where they met the remainder of the battalion. The arrival at Albert-square was witnessed by 7,000 spectators, who showed great interest in these men, who sacrifice their spare time to drill and thus prepare themselves against any Ipossible invasion. Some critics say that the Volunteer move- ment is a waste of time and money, but the very same critics passed the same compli- mentary remarks about our Territorials in peace time. When the testing time came the Territorials proved themselves a Godsend to England. They played a prominent part in getting the Hun on the run, and in keeping the good old flag free from disgrace. The war is not over yet, the Hun is not as strafed as he is going to be, and the Volunteer may yet be wanted; it is up to every man who an lift a rifle to prepare himself to defend his hearth I and home, and do his little bit.

===== 0002601_19160729_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
In Albert-square.

As the various units of the battalii;n assembled in Albert-square, they immediately formed up into column of companies in line, and after Sir Arthur Haworth, the; battalion commander, had conducted a few minor move- ments, they marched off, headed by the bat- talion band, which 'throughout the whole pro- ceedings played patriotic airs.

At Heaton Park.

The march from Albert-square to Beaton Park occupied about an hour, the route all the way being crowded with spectators, for the ' Volunteers' grey-green uniform was something new, and proved quite an attraction. On arriving at Heaton Park, the men were allowed a few minutes relaxation for the purpose of consuming their one ration carried, and right helrtily did they enjoy it, for the march had touched their appetites. As our representa- tive was walking round he overheard one grey- haired warrior remark. "Hey it's surprising what a bit of a march will do. Has anybody got anything left?" _ _ _

Each platoon was individually conducted round the trenches, and at each trench a N.C.O. from one of the various regiments stationed there was told off to explain and describe its numerous points that can be used to advantage. As the members of each platoon emerged frobi their tour of the trenches 'they were dismissed by their platoon commanders, and were ' then free for the remainder of the day, all so .much better off for knowledge of trench warfare that had been imparted.

===== 0002601_19160729_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE THOMAS READ.

Rush Geer Youth Diet) Of Wounds.

By. the death from wounds of Private Thomas Read, the Cheshire Regiment, in a field hospital, Lynam has added one more to its already large number of men 11+6 have paid the supreme sacrifice. This gallant soldier•was in the prime of life, having only just celebrated his twenty- first birthday somewhere in Franke. The sad news was received in a-letter sent to his mother at Rush Green, .and written by the Chaplain of the hospital in which he was being attended, and read as follows:

"It is with a sad heart that I am conveying the news of your son's death, which took place on July 11th, 1916. It was my duty to officiate at his funeral on the same day. He was admitted into hospital here and everything was done for him both by his doctor and nurses. His grave lies in the cemetery attached to. this town, and a cross has already been erected at its head. I know this letter will fill your heart with deep grief and cast dark shadows across your home. My heart goes out ,to you in tenderest sympathy. May the God of all con: solation be near to you in this time of trouble, nearer than he sorrow that 'hides in your heart, and nearer than, the tears thfit dim your eyes.—T. W. Hart, Chaplain." Private Thomas Read enlisted in September, 1914, and- was drafted out to France in Septem- ber of the following year. He was an exceed- ingly well-known local man, and was _educated at the Lymm Schools, and worshipped at the Congregational Church. He took an active in- terest in all sports, and was a member of the Lymm Lads' Club. Prior to the war he resided with his parents at Rush Green, and was employed at Bradshaw's Brickyard, Statham.

•

His brother, ,Private William Read, who was serving in the same regiment, is at present somewhere in France.

===== 0002601_19160729_art0142.txt | included=true | included =====
LANCE-CORPORAL W. JACKSON.

Home On Furlough.

Cheerful and breezy, and looking in the best of health, Lance-Corporal Wm. Jackson, of the Cheshire Regiment, one of our youngest soldier- heroes, arrived h;me on a short furlough last week-end. Though only slightly over 19 years of age at the present time, he has Tieen a soldier since the first days of the war, and has seen a considerable amount of active service some- where in France. Being a member of the Cheshire Regiment, he was mobilised at the outbreak of hostilities, and after training with his unit in various parts of the country, he was drafted to Franee in February, 1915, along with semeral other local men. Though practically onlY a boy, he played a man's part, and for several months experienced some of the keenest trench warfage possible, until he eventually got wounded, and sent over, once again to "Blighty." He is now training with his unit somewhere down South. It is interest- ing to know that since joining his unit in the South he has attained his first degree of pro- motion. In civil, life he is a gold beater, employed by Wm. Wright and Sons, gold beaters and refiners, of Newfields, and resides at Moston- street, Newfields. His only brother, Private Harry Jackson, is at present serving his countiy somewhere in France.

CORPORAL A. C. SAVILLE.

Noted Amateur Sportsman Wounded

was complimented on the smartness of his team by the General in command, who presented the medals.

In cricket he was a prominent player of our local team, and week by week he made for him- self new records and a greater name. His finest feat up. to the time of the war was 92 runs in 40 minutes. His bowling was as great as his batting, and although his present record -has slipped our memory, it is far above the average. In the season of 1913 he received his county cap and badge, and turned out for Cheshire against Lancashire at Old Trafford. In this match, which was his first appearance in first-class cricket, he delighted an enormous crowd of spectators by lifting the ball clean over the stands on several occasions.

Corporal Arthur Saville is a well-known Lvmm man, being a,Member of the Conservative Club. He was educated at the Manchester and Lymm Grammar Schools, and worshipped at the Lymm Parish Church, and for several years was a member of the choir. In civil life he practised as a commission agent, and represented the leather and rubber trades. Ile enlisted in th,, first month of the war, and was drafted out to France about nine months ago.

During his career with the Lyrani A.F.C., for which club :he kept goal, he- was selected captain of the team which represented the Lancashire and Cheshire Amateur League, and met the Belfast team at , Belfast just prior to the war. , In Army foot- ball he was no less famous, for he was selected daptain of his brigade team which, though running for two seasons, has never been defeated. In the first season they won the silver cup which was given by the Brigadier- General, and competed for at Epsom. For this feat he is the proud possessor of a silver medal. CHESHIRE VOLUNTEER REGIMENT

Lvmm Platoon.

Orders for week commencing Saturday, July 30th, 1916:— Tuesday, August lst.-7.30 p.m., Musketry; 8 p.m., Platoon Drill. Wednesday, August 2nd.-7.30 p.m., Musketry; Recruits only. Thursday. August 3rd.-7.30 p.in., Musketry; p.m., Platoon Drill. - By Order, G. R. WILLIAMSON. Platoon Commander.

Too!workers' Excursion.—The employes of Wm. Moss and Sons, the Lancashire Tool Works, Henry-street, had a brief rest from their strenuous work on Saturday last, and made Merry on the banks of the Dee at Chester. The party, which numbered well nigh forty, arrived at the hi&-,oric city about two o'clock, and while walking round the stately Walls chanced to meet a young Lymm soldier, Private Walter Mills, who acted as guide so ably that they were able to visit the principal points of interest. including the Castle, the headquarters of the Cheshire Regiment. The party returned in the late hours of the evening, feeling much better for their short relaxation, and ready to start on their important work. with renewed energy. May Queen Proceeds for Soldiars' Cigarettes.— The children have ever been in the fore of those realising what our gallant soldiers and saikors have sacrificed, and have always been ready

to lend a helping .hand to anything that might

add to the comtort of our gallant heroes. An instance has just ' been brought to our notice which though small in itself, gives us a slight idea of the spirit of patriotism and self-Sacri- lice which predominates in the hearts of our younger generation. A party of little children from the neighbourhood of Sandy-lane, Ought- rington, organised a small May Queen festival among themselves, and collected about, six shillings. This sum they spent on cigarettes, which they distributed amongst the wounded soldiers at the Brookfield Military Hospital last Sunday. Such acts as these " Tommy " will never forget, and as he goes once again into action, he will be happy in the thought that he faces the Hun with the blessings of the little mites that he is So gallantly defending.

===== 0002601_19160729_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
STOCKTON HEATH.

GIVER FREDERICK ROBINSON.

Gets D.C.M. For Bravery In The Dardanelles.

Gunner Frederick Robinson, R.F.A., who for some years previous to the war, lived at 10, Ellison-street, Stockton Heath, has been men- tioned in dispatches, and has been awarded the Distinguished Conduct Medal for conspicuous bravery in Gallipoli. It is rather a pity -that an independent account of the act which gained him this dis- tinction has not been sent to his relatives by one of his officers or comrades. His own refer- ences in letters are extremely meagre, and characterised by that extreme modesty which seems to afflict most of our heroes. Bluntly 'and plainly, the young man (he is only 25 years of age) with reckless bravery, carried shells to his gun at Chocolate Hill under heavy fire, and with ammunition exploding all about him. A couple of brief extracts from letters may explain the matter in some degree, and it may here be mentioned that after going through the terrible experiences of serving in Gallipoli he went to Egypt, and from thence to France, where he is at present in the firing line. Writing On June 18th to his step-mother, Mrs. Mullin, ;whom he always addresses in the most affectionate terms, he says :—" I have had rather good news to tell you. The Captain tells me I have got the D.C.M. I expect you will be sur- prised." A subsequent letter on July Bth, written when on board ship coming from Egypt, does not mention his great honour, butll'in one dated July 10th, in France, after referring to his splendid voyage and the fact that they did not "see any tin fish" en route, he says: "I won ithe D.C.M. on Chocolate Hill, Suvla Bay, Gallipoli, on November 7th, 1915; it was a Sunday morning."

In Very Hot Fire.

A further communication received in Stock- ton Heath last Sunday morning is a little More explicit The first thing I knew I was in Suvla Bay. I came with guns from my old battery and to brigade. We landed our four guns on C Beach, and then proceeded to Lela Baba. After being there about a week we got to Chocolate Hill—our section Wit on

D.C.M

the rig-nt. On November 7th, Surat -lay morning, the 'Turks started sending a few over until it got very hot. We were banging -away; the other guns out of action; all ammunition on fire. I kept on bringing the shells. I can tell you we had a very warm: time. T:key could not get direct hits on our gun; they were dropping all arourrd us. . . I will tell you more Then I get home. . . I ain wearing riiy ribbon which I received in Egypt. We are just behind the firing line." It is hoped he will have equally good fortune in France. .

===== 0002601_19160729_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
JAMES OR WILLIAM' ?

Mother's Anxiety Concerning A Dead

Son.

"Just a line to let you know that I found the body of your dear son, and very sorry for your trouble. I and my pals buried him as tidily as we could. He had nothing on him", only his pay book; that is the way we found out who he was." This letter was written on the 13th Ally in France by Private P. Cadweil, of a Pioneer Force, to Mrs. Jane Lawson, of Norcott Brook, Whitley. Now this heartbroken mother has two sons, both in the 'Cheshires, and it will be seen that in the letter just quoted no Christian name was given. Was it James or Willie? That was the mentally reiterated query' for three or four days, until a field card written on the 17th was received from the second &In which 'showed that he, at any rate, waS safe. Tip to Monday last Mrs. Lawson had not received an official communication as to the fate of her son, James, but the Vicar of Whitley (Rev. J. C. Trarapleasure) wrote the War Office for definite information. The last Iletter from James was dated 6th July, and in a comparatively brief note couched in hopeful terms, he stated that he and Bill were all right. James is 23 years of age, and William 21, and they were both attested men, being called up in their different groups about the same time. They were born in Whitley, and worked on an adjacent farm' owned by Mr. Mounfield. Mrs. Lawson is a widow, and on the death of her husband she was left with a family of nine young children to care for. Later in the week letter was received by Mrs. Lawson, confirming the sad tidings of the death of her son, Private James Lawson. His officer has written.:—" Your son was killed by a shell during a heavy bombardment, death being instantaneous. He was a very promising soldier, always willing, cheerful and able. Please accept my deepest sympathy in your sad bereavement."

===== 0002601_19160729_art0152.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE W. HOUGHTON.

" Gassed " In February And Shot In

July.

On Wednesday morning Mrs. Houghton, 2, Kimberley Drive, Stockton Heath, received a letter from her husband, Private W. Houghton, East Surrey Regiment, who was wounded in the big advance on July Ist, stating that he "is doing fine." Private Houghton, who is at pre- sent in a London hospital, received a bullet through the left wrist, the'bones of which were smashed. This is his second time for being in hospital during the present year. In February last he was "gassed" and had to undergo medical treatment, first at Netley- Hospital and sub- sequently at Epsom. Private Houghton ,was born at Wellfield- street, and was educated at St. 13arnaba„s' School. He is 29 years of age, and had been employed at the Wilderspool Brewery. He first of all joined the Royal Garrison Artillery, from which he transferred to the East Surrey Regiment. It will be remembered that this gallant regiment went into action kicking four footballs in front of them. There are three brothers of Private Hough- ton also in the Army, and his sister's husband has been wounded. Mrs. Houghton's brother, Private George Andrews, of the Cheshire Regi- ment, a Stockton Heath man, is serving in France.

ANNUAL TREATS.

Hill CC' ihfield Br eanp' tsisEt nSjouynindaeynt S. 000 l The annual treat given to the children of Hill Cliffe Baptist Sunday School took place' on Monday. A field opposite the chapel was kindly lent by Mr. Holland, and a large number of scholars, with parents and friends, were pre- sent. Various games were enjoyed by the young people, and the refreshment stall had Bx:con- stant stream of patrons. An excellent tea-was provided in the schoolroom. The weather waS all that could be desired, a fact which made the day's outing particularly, pleasant. Many young ,people were missed (being away serving their country), but in. spite of the depletion in this direction ,the friends at Hill Cliff° rejoice that the work amongst the juvenile members is going on eso well. Rev. G. Curtis (pastor) and Mr. Joseph Moss (superintendent) *addressed the gathering very briefly before breaking up, and a very happy day was brought to a close by a hymn of praise to the Giver of every good and perfect gitt. -

WESLEYAN SUNDAY SCHOOL. The annual treat in connection with the Wesleyan Sunday School was held on Thursday afternoon. The scholars assembled at the schoolroom at two o'clock and proceeded ta a field kindly lent by Mr. T. C. Williams. in Belvoir--road. They were in charge of Mr. C.

Wilcockson, one of the superintendents, and several teachers, and the secretary,, Mr. 4. Whitfreld. The children soon began to enter heartily into the usual games of rounders, foot- ball, cricket, swinging, &c. At 4.30 the party adjourned to the schoolroom, where tea wits - served by the lady workers. After tea games -

were resumed, and each class had its customary races, Mr. W. Twiss and Mr. G. Gould being in charge of these .interesting events. The tug of war, gents v. ladies was keenly contested, this being the event of the evening. The ladies ultimately triumphed, the young men being greatly missed. A troupe of donkeys added much to the amusement of the day, both young and old indulging in a ride round the field on these patient animals. The weather was fine, and the singing of the National Anthem brought a very enjoyable treat to a close.

WOOLSTON.

SCHOOL CHILDREN'S TREAT.

'The annual Church of England Day School children's tea and sports took place on Satur- day. Seventy parents and friends sat down to tea in addition to the Day School children. The sports were afterwards held in a field kindly lent by Mr. .1. Warburton, Well Farm. Winners:—

Girls.—Over 11: Lily Platt, Annie Gatley. 9to 11: Alma Lythgoe, Hilda Snell. 7to : Florence Snell, Lilian Buckley. Under 7; Emily Austin, Nancy Howard. Boys.—Over 11: Alfred Jaines, John Mar- shall. 10 to 11: James Owen, Jos. 'fly. - Platt. Bto 10: John Platt, John Kay. Under 8; Harold Kay, Edward Jaines. Much amusement was caused by an adult race, in which blindfolded gentlemen were driven by ladies, and vice-versa. This event was keenly contested and called for no little skill on the part of the drivers in piloting the blind between groups of spectators, who were asked to stand in the course. Private Abel Platt, driven by Mrs. Collier, won the first race, and Miss Adela Ryder, driven by Private T. Buckley, the second. Mr. Ernest Platt and Mrs. Jaines ran second in each event.

The prizes • were presented by Miss Gorton, and Mr. Ryder (head teacher) thanked all. particularly the parents, for their presence and hearty support. He, was specially pleased that Private T. Buckley, who, before enlistment, was the secretary to the School Treat Com- mittee, had obtained leave of absence froni his regiment in order to be present. Messrs. G. A. Platt, W. Wainwright, S. Monks, _Jos. Houghton, E. Platt, and J. Stubbs formed the Sports Committee.

A PICTURISED ROMANTIC NOVEL BY ROY L.McCARDELL ADAPTED FROM THE SENSATIONAL -FLYING A- SERIAL FILM

BY ALEC J. BRAID.

racters in this story are purely imaginary, living SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING CHAPTERS.

Colonel Arthur Stanleigh and Judge Lamar Stan- leigh, cousins, of Virginia, are bitter enemies over the possession of The Diamond from the Sky, a massive jewel heirloom found centuries ago in a fallen meteor. Colonel Stanleigh, when a daughter is born, buys, the male heir of a gipsy king, that he may keep the jewel. He plans to rear Esther, the girl; in secrecy. Esther and The Diamond from the Sky are stolen by Hagar, the gipsy mother. Years later Hagar returns Esther and the diamond, but of the old group only Dr. Lee is living. Blair Stanleigh kills Dr. Lee and steals the jewel. Then Blair Stanleigh and Arthur Stanleigh, the false heir; fights a duel. Arthur, thinking he has killed his cousin, flees in a racing motor car. In his mad fiight the machine goes over a river bank, and that night the modn shines on a body floating down the stream. Arthur is found by Esther and Hagar and nursed to health. Hagar tells him his real identity. Arthur swears to make himself worthy of Esther, and leaves, taking the diamond, and meets Blair Stanleigh. Blair takes him to a grand ball, where they meet Vivian Marston. a New York belle, wearing the diamond. Hagar is there, too, telling fortunes. Vivian has just seated herself When a brawny forearm clutches The Diamond from the Sky. A greater"confusion than ever that caused by the loss of the diamond arose when the Sheriff of Fairfax arrives at the ball and demands the arrast of Arthur Btanleigh. He escapes, partly through the efforts of Hagar. and later finds work and refuge as a farm hand. Luke Lovell drops the diamond into a mail bag. The gem clings by i4s Clasp to a rent in the bag and is afterwards found by Quabba, the hunchback organ-grinder. He dos not retain it long; Clarence. his monkey, drops it in an owls nest. Arthur, working on a farm as "Joan Powell," masks himself and goes to the Fairfax tournament, where he wins the highest honour. He is betrayed by Blair Stanleigh, and amid great con- fusion the young Virginians fifht to bar Arthur's escape. He succeeds in getting away, and the same night "John Powell" leaves Farmer Smith 4t: seek good fortune elsewhere. Through the eff3rts of Tom Blake, detective. Hagar obains pr.ci that Blair Stanleigh killed Dr. Lee. She takes the Photograph of the .thumb-print to Mrs. Lamar Stanleigh, is brutally attacked .by Blair and her mind destroyed. A negro boy finds the diamond in the owl's /lest. The injury to Hagar's brain is so severe that her mind is quite in the past, and although taken by Esther to the old gipsy camp in the hope that association with old friends will restore her reason, it is a forlorn hope. Strap McGee, tramp, robs the negro boy of the diamond, and in an opium den is killed for the sake of the gem, which Hung Li hangs round an idol's neck. here it was seen by Blair and Vivian on a slum- ming visit, and the former-steals it from the idol's neck, a deed which is witnessed by Hung 'Li (who tries to cut off the hand of the marauder), Tom Blake, and the Blooms.

minister had thought to turn on the light. The detective, still dizzy from his stunning fall, roused himself and hurried from the house followed by the astounded Mr. Bloom. The taxicab of the newly-married pair was gone. The 'detective and the gambler ran to their vehicle in the next street, and, finding the bursted tyre had been replaced, ordered the driver to make for the depot.

On their part, the fugitives had not proceeded to the depot. It was the more subtle Vivian who sug- gested a daring scheme of escape from the city. They went to Ike Bloom, and, having bound and blindfolded the unfortunate man, disguised them- selves. Tom Blake and Abe Bloom, both wild with rage, were searching the outgoing train, when a natty college youth and a middle-aged gentleman of clerical aspect boarded the cars.

_ In the gipsy camp the proximity of the Romany people brought no return of mental health to the afflicted Hagar. In the trying days that followed the devoted Esther would have given way to despair but for the cheering presence of her new and faith- ful friend and - servant Quabba, the hunchback organ-grinder. Sedition was at work among Hagar's tribe. Everywhere Luke Lovell was whispering evil counsel. He had determined to wrest the reins of power falling from the hands of Hagar. Once leader of the gipsies. he felt that it would be an easy, thing to coerce Esther into marriage and thus be assured of his Romany kingship. Hagar's wealth was a proverb among the gipsies, and while they loved Esther, they listened to Luke when he whispered to them that now Hagar was bereft of her reason her wealth belonged to and should be shared by the tribe. Quabba sought in every way to cheer Esther. While Hagar sat in her van moaning and mutter- ing for a child, a son that none had ever heard had been born to her. Quabba would take Esther for walks upon the mountains, knowing Hagar was safe among the gipsies, attended by the elder women waiting and watching near by at such times. The favourite spot "where Esther and Quabba daily climbed to talk of Arthur and to wonder where he was, and when they would }Aar from him, was to the mountain top, where a great balancing rock swayed to the lightest touch and had menaced the valley below for centuries. Upon such occasions they tool field-glasses with _

them and would watch the roads for miles away, xpaidering if every distant rider were Arthur re- 'turning to them. One day Quabba turned the glasses upon the gipsy village in the hollow at the mountain's foot. There was some excitement in the camp, it was evident. The figure of Luke Lovell could be plainly seen haranguing the crowd. The gipsies had gathered around him and Luke was appealing to them. "Hagar has robbed you for years. Make me your king and I will share her treasure with you. I know where to find it." When Esther and Quabba pushed through the thick Luke had brought out the supposed treasure chest from the van without protest from the crazed Hagar. Esther stepped forward, and defied Lovell to open the box. "I have the key!" she cried, pro- ducing it from her bosom. "And you "—and she turned an indignant glance upon the gipsies— "have you no respect for your queen or for me and listen to the words of Luke Lovell? I will open the box." She did so, and brought out a time-stained document. "To be opened at my death in case my son, Arthur Stanleigh, should prove un- worthy of the Stanleigll name "—and the-document revealed the Stanleigh secret. Luke Lovell, as well, as Esther, now knew the truth. The would-be' gipsy king grinned at the knowledge, and then fled as the gipsies drove him from the camp. What he knew would keep until he could sell the secret, but ,he was determined to revenge himself upon the in- sult of exclusion. Meeting a number of tramps he aroused their criminal cupidity with his tale of gipsy wealth, and they followed him to raid the camp. From his vantage point ,by the rocking-stone

Quabba saw the attack, and: seizing a pine branch, the hunchback, mad at the sight of Lovell drag- ging Esther from the van, pushed the stone over. Gathering impetus with every foot the rolling rock

- _ brought with it an avalanche of stone and rubble and overwhelmed the camp. _ _ _

_ Afflicted as he was with his deformity. Quabba the hunchback—he of the sunny face and happy heart—was as agile and as sinewy as the monkey Clarence. his companion on his way‘through the world. But now the hunchback was neither sunny of face nor happy of heart. A wild tremor of fear, anxiety and remorse shook him in an ague of terror and confusion.

Sending the rocking stone, prised from the perch where it had swayed for Centuries, had only meant death for all below, thought Quabba. Instead of saving his young and old mistress and his gipsy friends from the raid of desperate tramps led by Luke Lovell, Quabba now deemed that he had destroyed those he had loved as well as their enemies. - _

But as he ran panting dov3ll the mountain side Quabba saw that some of the gipsies, warned by the clatter and roar of the landslide the massive, bounding, loosened rocking stone had started, had fled to safety. He saw some half-score of gipsy men and women toiling rapidly up the opposite slope of the valley from the destroydd gipsy 'camp. Through the dust that was settling in a cloud over the debris and rubble where the camp had stood the straining eyes of Ouabba could Mark the 'ragged figures of some of the assaulting tramps limping away from the scene of destruction, as bootless as they had come.

Away in the west "John Powell" overheard a gang of train robbers 'planning to hold up the Southern Pacific express, the train on which Blair and Vivian were fleeing. Determined to warn the train he galloped to the track and climbed aboard. 13ut the guard and conductors paid no-heect to the hysterical man who pantingly told his story. They did not believe him• and flung him off. (To be continued.)

===== 0002601_19160729_art0163.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAPTER IX. A RUNAWAY dIATCH,

While the flames were licking round the many treasures of Hung Li's den, reducing, to a smouldering ruin the resort of evildoers, Blair Stanleigh hurried back to Vivian. Confident in his Pluck the girl knew that only death could place a barrier between him and possession of the diamond. Sure enough he had succeeded. From her bedroom window she watched his stealthy approach, and a few minutes later he had clambered to the window. "Vivian. the diamond is mine. See, here it is. At last we have the treasure."

"Give it to me. Blair. let me once again clasp it around my neck, the beautiful jewel." "No, the diamond has its price. You promised to marry me if I got it, and you must, or I shall keep it." Blair resolved upon the only price he would accept for the proud possession of the Stanleighs—the favour of a woman he loved. " Quick, say if you agree." "Yes," nodded Vivian. "Then move quickly because Blake and Bloom are sure to come after me. I know they are after the diamond." So did Vivian, because she had been present when the syndicate for its recovery was formed. "I will wait for you," said Blair, tensely. In a few minutes Vivian had joined him. A few blocks from the Randolph house they hailed a belated taxi, and were driven to the home of the Rev. John Gray, noted in conservitive Richmond as "the marrying minister." _ .

Blake and Abe Bloom were hot upon his track, as Blair anticipated, and had roused Mrs. Randolph within a few minutes of the flight of the lovers. A search of the house revealed the hasty flight of Vivian, and assuming that they had gone to see "the marrying minister," Blake hurried to call upon Mr. Gray, remarking to the flurried Abe,. "I have a wedding present—a pair of bracelets— for the groom." But the detective was too late. Blair and Vivian were man and wife before the unceremonious burst- ing in of the detective confirmed the minister's fears that he was assisting the escape of young people afraid to ".face the music." Stepping forward Blake, with a deftness acquired by long practice, snapped the handcuffs on the bridegroom, exclaiming, "We want the diamond, and I arrest you for the murder of Dr. Lee." Vivian did not scream; she clutched the diamond and drew it to her breast. Blair, roused to a frenzy of fright and rage at the thought of losing Vivian and of being hauled off as a murderer, swung his manacled hands with all his strength. For once Tom Blake was caught napping. He was knocked back over a chair and lay a moment stunned. Abe Bloom was next to feel the swinging double blow of the infuriated Blair. Vivian, quick as thought, pressed the button of the electric light switch near the door, and she and Blair rushed from the room, Blair pausing just inside the hall to draw back the sliding door. They dashed from the place, and 'were in their taxicab and away before the excited and irresolute

IRISHMEN GO TO IRCAM VIA WARRINGTON.

When two Irishmen were charged at the Warrington ,Munitions Conft on Monday withl beilig absent without leave on the 24th June, a departmental manager of a works at Irlam said that the defendants only stayed three, days and then cleared off.

Mr. Brassington, of the National Union of Railwaymen, who represented one of the de- fendants, said that both of them were now in Dublin again. Along with eighteen other men they were in the Dublin Labour Exchange on the 21st June, when the officials -referred them to an advertisement by the Irlam firm for labourers at 7d. to Bd. per hour, with 7s. a week war allowance. They all decided to cross the rater, and went to the works via the Warring- ton Labour Exchange. When they got there, however, they could not find any department where the wages were as high as advertised, and gradually the men dwindled away until there were only the two defendants left. Being abso- lutely. stranded they decided to work for 6d. nn hour and war bonus in order to get their fare back to Dublin. That was all they stayed for. The case was adjourned for a week. ,

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JULY 29, 1916. IN THE COURTS.

THE KNiFE.

Illeged Serious Assault Out Bewsey

Way.

Edward Walsh, 14, Ellen-street, was remanded for a week at the Borough Police Court cn Monday on a charge of stabbing John Bennett, 75, Pierpoint-street, in the thigh, and inflicting: a dangerous wound. Chief Constable Nicholls said the prosecutor was unable to attend, as he could not get doWn stairs, and they had not taken the statement of the medical man. It appeared that the prosecutor, the prisoner, and other men were having refreshment in the Imperial Hotel, and an argument started after they had left the public-house and reached Catherine-street. The prisoner was seen de- liberately to take a knife frotn his pocket and stab the other man through the thigh. The blade passed through his clothes and Bennett felt the blood trickling down his leg. Some witnesses seized the prisoner's wrist and took the knife from him. •When charged at the Police Station prisoner made this statement: "That was an aggravated assault. I had to defend myself. He 'was slapping my face and he shook me. , I told him if he did it again I would put the knife in him. He did do it again and 'he called 'nap a dirty hound and I put the knife in him."

===== 0002601_19160729_art0168.txt | included=true | included =====
"A TISSUE OF LIES."

Bench Resent Manchester Doctor's Warmth.

After Inspector Forster, at the Borough Court on Thursday had proved a motor offence against Dr. Charles T. Hawkins, of Cross-lane, Gorton, Manchester, he said that the Way the doctor treated him was most ungentlemanly when he, stopped the car in Sankey-street. "In all my long experience in the Police Force I have never met a motorist who -was so uncivil," he declared. ,

Dr Hawkins replied that this allegation against his conduct was nothing hut a tissue of lies, and it was the Inspector who had acted brutally and pettishly. Chief Superintendent Williamson protestel against the expression "tissue of lies," and said that if the defendant had any complaints to make in regard to the conduct of the Inspector he had his remedy with the Chief Constable.

The defendant reiterated the expression and, in, fining him 205., the Chairman (Mr. J. Rymer Young) said that the Bench very mu:th resented the ..unnecessary warmtk which • the defendant had introdUced into the case.

===== 0002601_19160729_art0169.txt | included=true | included =====
FOR HEROIC "HUSBAND'S SAKE."

On the appeal of the Chairman (Councillor Henry Roberts) and Mr. T. S. Steel, solicitor, for a complainant at the Borough Police Court on Tuesday, the young widow of a soldier apologised to the wife of a Sergeant in the R.F.A. Thr a common assault upon her. Mr. Steel mentioned that the defendant had a grievance against the complainant because her husband had not yet gone to the 'Front, and that was the origin of the trouble.

When appealed to for an apology the defend- ant, who was weeping and dressed in mourn- ing, said, "I don't see why I should apologise when my husbantl did his bit." The Chairman pointed out , that the only alternative to that would be a conviction if the charge were proved, and after a time she agreed to the suggestion by remarking, "Well, I will apologise for my husband's sake, but it was through his name I did it."

War Fund Handicap, FIRST ROUND.
