MILITARY TRIBUNALS.

CONSCIENCE MEN.

Weil-known Objectors Meet

With Refusals.

Two well-knowai conscientious objectors appeared before the Warrington Tribunal on Tuesday. Wilfred H. Sanders, aged 29, asked the Tri- bunal to reverse their former decision, setting him apart for non-combatant service. He had bfen employed Cin a railway. The Clerk .(Mr. Hallaway) : The railway com- pany did not release you. • Applicant said they had since released him, and he wished to hand in a lengthy document for the Tribunal to read. , He said it would prove that he was released because he had not attested.

The Clerk said that had nothing to do with the Tribunal. • If he had any grievance with the railway company he could settle it with 'them. _

Applicant said he had ailplied to be referred to tb Pelham Committee for work of national importance. The Tribunal, after a brief consultation, re- fused to reverse their decision.

Applicant: I am given to understand that the Government have now Made arrangements for anyone who does not feel disposed to accept non- combatant service to be transferred to the Pelham Committee.

The Chairman (Dr. Joseph): We don't reverse our decision; that rests with the Government.

Applicant: I understand the case is being considered.

The clerk: The decision will be registered. You can appeal against it in Liverpool. Applicant iaid that a member of the Govern- ment had taken up the case. Military Representative (Captain Mathams): What member of the Government?

Applicant: The matter was put in the hands of Mr. Philip Snowden. The Chairman: He is not a member of the Government. _ _

Applicant: You won't hear my appeal? The Chairman: We have heard it. We don't reverse our decisions. An Ultra Socialist.

A prominent member of the Trades and Labour Council, in the person- of Thomas Howard,' aged 26, unmarried, applied for exemption on the 'ground that he was employed on work of national importance, and further on account of conscientious objections. He was in temporary employment about three months, and he was now employed as a farm labourer. He handed in a certificate which passed him for garrison duty at home or abroad. -In reply to the Clerk, he said he was a conscientious objector; and -did not belong to any religious body. Since he was last before the Tribunal he had reviewed his own ease, and was even more definite in his views now than before. He thought- the Trilipmal would also review theirs.

• The Clerk: They did not agree with you at Liverpool? Applicant: No, , they gare it consideration; but, of course, I don't .agree with the verdiet. (Laughter.) The Clerk: Are you -prepared to do anything to assist? •

Annlicant : Well, I consider arn doing so now on the land. Captain Mathams :Yo said that you owed no duty to filo' country. , - Applicant: It is essential for us all to live that, we produce food stuffs. Captain Mathams : There is a difference be- tween the country and the land. Applicant: Yes, the world is the land.

Th. e Clerk: You don't believe in any God, do you?—No, I don't. Applicant said he was of more use in agri- culture than he would be in defending any property. Tinnion: Your application is re- fused.

Annlicant: js that on both grounds? Alderman Tinnion: Yes. ' Applicant then procured azi -appeal form. Estate Manager Exempted.

An appeal was made by T. and J. L. Tunstall and Co. on behalf of the mtungier of the estate department of their firm. Mr. Pemberton, who appeared;stated that be- fore the war the staff comprised 12 persons, six of military age. Three had enlisted and two had been rejected; two others would be eligible for service in the autumn. The 'manager of the estate department, who was an attested married man, was essential to the conduct of the busi- ness. Without him they could not carry on. He had the management of several important trust estates, and over a thousand houses and business premises in Warrington, Liverpool. Manchester, Widnes, Earlestbwn, and the villages adjacent to Warrington, in addition to a number of farms in Lancashire and Cheshire. He had been with the firm for over 24X years, and his technical knowledge was invaluable. He also acted as an auctioneer and valuer. Owing to the complexity of increased taxation and the consequent number of new assessments and reclaims, the work of other departments had increased enormously, and all were working at high pressure. Conditional exemption was granted. Licensee Exempted.

Mr. A. Browne, solicitor, appeared for the licensee of a Warrington inn, and informed the Tribunal that his client had been eight years in business. He was 37 last January, ,and his wife had had to undergo a serious operation which rendered it impossible for her to under- take the management of the, business. He had no male assistants of any description, and from figures submitted by Mr,, 13rowne, trade appeared to be in a flourishing condition. He was willing to give as much time as he could to the Volunteer Force, and had had three months' preparatory training'. In reply to the military representative, applicant said he was willing to do munition work in spare time, if necessary. Conditional exemption was granted. Application Refused.

A grocer, tobacconist, newsagent and: general dealer, carrying on business at Liverpool-road, explained his affairs to the Tribunal with the object of showing that he was entitled to total exemption. He is 36 years of age, and is married, with three children. There are two shops, one being carried on by his wif.l, and there is a long term to run under the lease of the premises. The application was refused. . Holders of Tied House Licences.

Mr. T. S. Steel, solicitor, who representen the licensee of premises owned by a large brewery, was informed by the Tribunal that the hearing of this and similar eases would be adjourned until they learned the views of the firm on the possibility of having the licences -transferred to the wives of present holders.

. , ing the appeal were not now what they were when exemption was granted. Captain Mathams said that -the Government is 'controlling supplies of leather, comrhandeer- ing it and controlling tin" sales, and only allowing manufacturers to obtain leather upon certificates from Government. departments. He. contended that the factor's business could not be sufficiently important to warrant the con- ditional exemption of 'a young man. Mr. Browne said that the circular by the devernment came into operation on the 2nd May last, and the applieation was made on the 30th May, when conditional exemption was granted. Figures were given then showing the turnover, and he could now give theip figures showing that it had gone higher since then. When Conditional exemption was granted the father entered into a large contract with a firm making boots for the -Russian Army to• supply them with leather. ,

The Tribunal reserved theiy decision. Conditional Exemption.

Conditional exemption was granted to the following:— .

A grocer and provision dealer, aged 39 years, married, three children, who explained that he had conducted the busipess for 14 years. It had been carried on by his late father for 26 years. The only assistance applicant got was from a boy on Saturdays. .

wholesale hardware dealer carrying on a general business. He is 38 years 'of age and a number of people were dependent on the busi- ness, his wife and her mother, his father, two unmarried sisters and his youngest ,sister, who is married, and whose husband has joined the Army. Husband of a coal dealer who acts as her manager and works the whole concern.

Upholsterer Temporarily Exempted.

The proprietor ,of a-n upholstering and news- agency business applied for exemption. A married man with two children, and aged 33. years. he stated that he formerly had two rac:n working for him. One had gone to the ~war and the other, who was called up on June Ist, had been given two months' exemption. Applicant's father was absolutely dependent, on him, and he kept the newsa7ency business. going for his benefit. He was 'doing upholstering fer three firms in town, and when the Lighting Order came out he fixed 1,500 blinds for them.

- Exemption for three m,nths wip granted. Corn Merchant's nusiness.

A corn merehapt and farmer asked for exemp- tion for himself and four of his employes. He had an appeal in on behalf of a fifth man, but the latter had joined up. One man (28) managed the corn mill and did all the grinding and crushing, and also worked the chaff-cutting machine. Another man was his teamsman, and the third (36) he described as his under study. He was himself already in the Volunteer Training Corps. The employer and the Iwo latter men were granted conditional exemption, while the re- maining man was given a month. Military Appeal to Reconsider.

KEEPING UP BUSINESSES.

At Thursday's sitting of the Warrington Tribunal, 'a coal dealer, aged 20 years, whose former certificate expired on the 31st May, applied for further exemption. His brother (17) earns 13s.' a sister (18) t•. at Messrs. Peter Walker and Son, and a second sister (14) Ss. 6d. a week at the Cockhedge Factory. He lives with an uncle and aunt and pays half the rent. His earnings average a week. One month's further exemption was granted in order to give a pnlicant an opportunity of disposing of his business: A Builder's Appeal.

Mr. T. F. Wallington, builder, appealed on behalf of two employes. a joiner and a brick- setter. He explained that before the war he had four joiners and four bricklayers con- stantly at work. He had a second bricklayer at present, an attested man, who had been rejected by the Medical Board. He had con- tracts for work for controlled firms and had been so shorthanded for some time past that the men were putting in eighty hours a week. Conditional exemption was granted in the case of the joiner and the bricklayer • was allowed three months. . Insurance Officials.

The cases of several insurance officials—dis- trict superintendents for well-known companies —were adjourned for consideration by the Tribunal.

===== 0002601_19160729_art0195.txt | included=true | included =====
Dairy Farmer's Employe.

The proprietor of a dairy farm applied for exemption for a driver, aged 36, of an engine used for a milk-separating machine. - Three hundred gallons of milk were used in a day and sometimes they made as much as 24 cwt. of butter.

Conditional exemption was granted on the understanding that the man joined the Volun- teer Training Corps. Tea Company's Manager.

Three months' exemption was granted to the manager of the Bridge-street branch of ,the National Tea Company. It was stated that, in addition to tea, general groceries, provisions and tinned goods were also sold. The firm had taken over the 'business of the Queen Tea Co. and the man in question was particularly con- versant with all the local conditions. Exemption For Laundry Hands.

Mr. F. H. Swallow (solicitor) appeared for the manager of the Thelwall-lane Laundry, who asked .for exemption in favour of three employes—one the washhouse foreman, and the other two men in charge of the motor delivery vans. Mr. Swallow pleaded that the men were indispensable to the business.

Conditional exemption was granted to the foreman and three months each to the motor van drivers.

Other Conditional Exemptions.

Most of the applications were either put forward on business and domestic grounds or by employers on behalf of men who they stated were indispensable to them in different capacities. .

'Amongst the applicants granted conditional exemption were an insurance agent, a green- grocer and pedlar, a fish salesman, fruiterer with contracts for military and public institu- tions, man emnloyed on farm of 74 acres, fore- man in a cycle repairing establishment, and the cutter in a tailoring business.

APPEAL COURT.

Marine Store Dealers Meet With

Refusal.

Two single men, aged ,29 and 24 years, who help in the carrying on of a hair, skin and metal business in Allen-street, Warrington, went to the Appeal Colirt at Liverpool on Thursday with the request that the refusal of exemption by the local Tribanol should be reversed. Mr. T. S. Steel was their solicitor. It was stated that three of the five sons carried on the business in Allen-street, this having been Oft to their mother, who was now in a very precarious state of health, having been in bed for twelve month. They had an extensive business, having dealings with 90 per cent. of the tanners in England. The hair they sold was used by the Government in hospitals. This year they had contracted for many thousands of pounds' worth of goods, and if the contracts were not fulfilled the firm would be liable for a breach. They did a large export

trade, and only last week they made a shipment of £2,000 worth of goods to the U.S.A. They were one of the largest deatzrs in skins in--the country,..and; 'special knoliledge, bang- required, they we-re both ~.indispensable. At the 'prese monent they had on hind 30,000 Skins, whi -11 .had to be elassilik,d, If the one .aged 2:9 had to go into. ,the Army 'their export ,trade' would come to an end.- They ethployed between 30 and .40 women.: Before the war they -had 10 men, but now they only had three. Applicants added -that they had no desire to, escape ,their liabilities to servo.' their _ country, their reason -for appelding being because they ..felt they . were, doing their duty in 'their .business. If they went -it would .m2an financial ruin to them besides. _ _ .

In the notes forwarded by the Warrington Tribunal it was stated that "These two eases present softie peculiar features." They then detailed the various phases of the business which all the brothers. conducted, and ended by declaring that. the Tribunal was of opinion that the twgh applicants could be spared, and recommendiffl; that a brother who had recently gone to Liverpool should return to 'Warrington. This One had ben in Ireland lately, and since been rejected by the Medical Board. The Military Representative (Captain Dickin- son) reinarked that that case had been before the local, 'Tribunal three times, and had caused a great deal of trouble in getting out the 'acts. Mr. Steel produced a letter from the brother referred to in Liverpool, stating that he now had no .connection with the firm it Warrington. _ _

The Elder Applicant: That brothel- 'left twelve months ago—in August of last year. He used to manage the business at Warrington. The Military Representative: At the local Tribunal you said he, left about Christmas. What made him leave you? Applicant: Strained relations. The Lord Mayor: Were you all four partners? —We had a mutual arrangement until then. Did you have equal shares ?—Yes. How did you share the profits?—Mother took 50 per cent. and we four divided the remainder. The Military representative again pointed out that there were here five brothers all of military age, and not one of them was serving. He then asked :the elder applicant when the brother referred to actually left the business at War- rington ? Elder Applicant: In August. Have you anything to prove that? What did he get when he left?—Half his share. • How much did you pay him?

• The Elder Applicant then wrote dcwn an amount, which he handed to the Lord Mayor. The Military Representative: This brother has still a half share in the business?—No: We paid him the balance later. When?—ln June. .

The Military Representative: There is no proof of that. Wo ought to have receipts to show• that the money was handed- 3ver. Mr. Steel emphasised that the business was an extensive one, and there were .contracts on the books for £7,000. The Elder Brother pointed out thnt he was responsible for all financial matters, and if he left there would be no one to trace debtors. lie added that the other applicant was yard manager. The Tribunal retired to consider their de- cision and, after five minutes' absence, the Lord Mayor said, "Appeal dismissed. We think they ought to go."

===== 0002601_19160729_art0203.txt | included=true | included =====
Ironmongery Manager.

Mr. H. J. Westbrook put forward the case of a lady who appealed for her son (34), who managed an ironmongery business, consisting of stalls in the Market and shops at Bank Quay and Stockton Heath. Having admitted that one son had been exempted because of his mechanical knowledge, Mr. Westbrook urged that it was absolutely necessary that the manager should be permitted to stay if the busi- ness were to be carried on well. The Lord Mayor pointed out that at the local Tribunal it was intimated by Mr. Gough, one of the executors, that the appellant's services were dispensable. "In view of that we cannot let this man off," he said. • A Precocious Merchant.

Mr. T. S. Steel represented a Mersey-street iron and metal broker, who appealed for his nineteen-year-old son, declaring his indispensa- bility. The father was unable to read or write. They were engaged on conti'acts for munition works, and, therefore, were doing work of a national character.

The Lord Mayor said the local Tribunal were of opinion that the son's age precluded any claim to indispensability, being convinced that the work he did was really that of 'a clerk.

In reply to the Lord Mayor, the son said he did a lot of estimating and buying. The Lord Mayor: What have you bought?— Several big things. Not very long. ago I. bought a boat at Liverpool with seven cranes on it. A nurse was drowned on its last voyage. Did you buy the nurse?—(Laughter.) Appeal dismissed. Fish Dealer's 500 Customers.

The owner of a fish shop in Orford-lane, who 'also goes hawking, said he had about '5OO customers altogether, and if he went into the Army he would have to give up his business. The Chairman told him that his wife could look after the stable portion of the business, and he might make arrangements for the hawking to be done by someone else. They allowed him a fortnight—which, taking into account the fortnight allowed by the military, means a month—in which to settle his affairs. The appeal of a greengrocer having a shop in Latchford, wils dismissed. He had been granted two months' exemption by the local Tribunal from the 4th July.

===== 0002601_19160729_art0204.txt | included=true | included =====
Workhoine Contractor.

A clothier and credit draper asked for an ex- tension of the three months allowed him by the local Tribunal. He said he had a turnover of £7OO per year. and had a contract with the Workhouse. His wife was in ill-health.

The Military Representative: She has de- veloped that since you were before the local TribunalF—Yes, that's natural. (Laughter.) Appellant then recanted the joke by stating that his wife had been in delicate health for a long time. The Tribunal decided to allow him leave to abpeal again in October. In the meantime he was to make any arrangements which he could for the carrying on of his work.

These wonderful animals are said to do everything but talk, and even attempt' to do that. At the termination of the performance the Girl and the Seal enter the tank together and present some attractive evolutions.

It may lack g°r: geousness of scenery, but then; it escapes an,/ allusion to being gaudy. Concentration is mu",e on the leading artistes themselves, and thongs there is an entertaining surplus of chorus girls; they are not *allotted too Much to do. A1:1 occasional -chorus and dance and then a long sit-down, with cigarettes between their lips and side-lcng glances from their eyes inaintuin their' attractiveness •unimpaired.

In fact, they form a pleasing and approving semi-circle to the principals, who come along and contribute fascinating items of. coined.), or music, or thusical comedy, after, the manner of a drawing-room entertainment in the Vire6t End.

The singing of Miss Nan Foster is superb and the dancing of the Dorane Sisters ana Wood is wonderfully light, unique and artistic, notably that of the "Parisian Pony Trot."

But the "star" of the production is Miss Phillis St. Clair, who succeeded J.liss Glyder, the famous American comedienne, 0: few weeks ago. "Young and blonde, she has all the attractiveness requisite for a leading ladY. Her voice is pleasant and she installs character into her songs. Her dancing is light and well polished; and I daresay she ranks as one of 'the best recent products of Mr., .lOhn dancing academy in London. Dominating all are a vivacity and a subtlety redolent of the brightness and cleverness of her temPerament- -

Miss St. Clair •has a promising' firtar Achievement should accrue from any ambltio_2ll she has. For twelve months she under-studieu, Miss Glyder and once went on the stage vritf: out a rehearsal. CYCLING AND MOTORING. A WEEK-END IN YORKSHIRE. The Yorkshire dales' offer many inducements to those-who are in search of a cycling holiday. The roads are extremely good; the scenery varies to a remarkable degree; and while there is a goodly number of hills encountered there are few Which are - at ,all dangerous. The complete trip, through re- markably fine dale country, and the distances are as follows :—Leeds to Otley, 11 miles; Ilkley, 4f; Bolton Bridge, 51; Pateley Bridge. 101; Ripon, 11 masham, 91; Wensley, 111; Hawes, 15; linker. 8; Heeth, Si; Richmond, 104; Rokeby, 131; Gainford, 91; Darlington. 8; total, 1361 miles. There are many other dales which may be visited, some of which possess special charm, but I have in- cluded in the above all the best of the scenery and the most suitable cycling roads. THE MONTHLY OVERHAUL. If you want to obtain the Maximum amount of pleasure from your bicycle you should make it a rule carefully to examine all the working parts once or twice a season. This is the way, and really the only way, to assure good running. It doesn't occupy much time to look over your machine, but even if it took ten times as long, it is worth if. All the bearings, valve tubing, brake blocks, &c., should come in for their share of attention. Only on rare occasions will it be found necessary to take the bearings to pieces. As a rule I find it, sufficent to flood them, with paraffin oil. By this means they Can be cleaned thoroughly, and if a little good lubri- cant be used afterwards the bearings will run per- fectly. At the same time the tyres should be in- spected. It is surprising how the life of a tyre can be lengthened if a little stout canvas be used here and there. Nuts, cotter pins, (tc., should be screwed up tightly, if the machine is to withstand the vibration caused by uneven roads. And the roads are very bad in some places just now. The fact that our roads are as good as they are speaks volumes for the excellent way the foundations were laid in years gone by. THE VALUE OF GLASSES. A great deal of prejudice exists among cyclists against the use of spectacles, and several notes have appeared lately in some of our contemporaries con- demning their use in quite a wholesale manner. But it seems to me to be a matter which must be left entirely to the individual to decide. Sometimes I use them; sometimes I don't. On a very bright or a windy day they certainly add greatly to one's com- fort. For anybody who suffers from weak eyes they are a source of very great comfort and protection in that they preserve the eyes from dust and wind. Particularly at this time of the year the number of small flies and insects in the air is considerable, and glasses prevent them from coming in contact with the eyes. They certainly do possess one disadvan- tage, and that is when the ;weather is wet or misty the lenses become clouded and sqmetintes it is diffi- cult to see through them. But this is the very time when they are least needed. EXPLOSIONS IN THE SILENCER. I don't know which is the more annoying—explo- sions in my own silencer or teirrific reports emanat- ing from a passing motor cycle. Each is equally annoying to the pedestrian, and is very liable to earn the motor cyclist more hate than love. The cause, of these explosions is quite a simple one. It is owing to the fact that a mixture too weak to fire passes from the cylinder into the silencer, and re- mains there until the next charge of hot gas fires it with-a bang. Thus it usually happens that ex- plosions will take place after descending a hill ac the engine is switched on again, the silencer hav- ing accumulated quite a respectable store of explo- sive. Obviously, the way to prevent the accumu- lation of gas would be to close the throttle before commencing the descat, but if you do this the re- sulting suction usually drags a good deal of oil up into the combustion chamber, sooting the plug and causing clouds of smoke. The right way is to three- parts close the throttle and open the air wide, and then any gas that accumulates in the silencer is too weak to fire. ON CHANGING GEAR. Very many motor cyclists have a great deal to learn with regard to the art of gear changing. In nine cases out of ten it is with a bit of a clank and a rattle and an unpleasant jerk of the machine' that the new gear is thrust home. Watch the next motor cycle you see mount a hill and prove it for your- self. Some of the principal faults with two or three speed motor cyclists are they change down either too early or too late, causing the machine to slow down with a jerk or labour after the change. as the case may be. They abuse the privilege of changing down or up without raising the exhaust. This is allowed by most makers, but is none the less foolish. They bang the gears in, instead of sliding them in and waiting for the " bite " before completing the change.. They throw a very heavy strain on engine and gear after free-wheeling gown a hill. Avery motor cyclist who is not handy with his gears would be well advised to spend an afternoon ascending and

- of descending the nearest hill. which gives plent9 opportunity for change speed business, concentri:;, ing his attention not on making sp'ectacular Si' ascents, but on the gear changing. A CURE FOR LEAKY TYRE VALVES. Whenever a tyre requires pumping up even, ost days make it a: rtile to examine,the valvo. invariably this is where the trouble lies. Look 'O, at the rubber washer to be found in the screw Ito which has to be detached every time the Porriflittle to be attached, not the big dust cap. This poor) washer, owing to the squashing it gets-, is veri„; of pressed out of shape and cut about by the -Cwp of the valve, and, nine times out of ten, is the cOsierki the leak. I can never understand why all 111 tbe do not fit washers of hard leather on top rubber washers, these I have found quite obvi,a tbe all the trouble. Mr. S. • Johnson, formerly a member of the Warrington Police Force, and Mrs. Johnson, of. 41, Clifton-street, have received news that their nephew, Rifleman James Henry Johnson, of the Rifle Brigade, died on Thursday, the 13th inst., from wounds received during the big advance. On July Bth, an Army Chaplain, writing for Rifleman Johnson, said the latter had been wounded and hoped to be in England before long. The Chaplain added a postscript sav- ing, "Your nephew is very seriously ill, though to-day there is an improvement." On July 16th, from.. the 12th Stationary

H. JOHNSON. Died of Wounds.

Hospital, the same Chaplain wrote :—" I deeply regret to say that James passed peacefully away on Thursday afternoon. He was laid to rest with full military honours on Friday afternoon in a little cemetery. He bore his sufferings with unflinthing courage and patience, and endeared himself to us all. It was my privilege to give him his Communion on Wednesday morning, and he was very happy. Please accept our deepest sympathy with you in your loss." Rifleman Johnson, who was an orphan, came from Bolton, of which town he was a native, almost seven years ago, and had since made his home with his uncle and aunt. He was 23 years of age, and enlisted immediately upon the out- break of war. He had been in France fourteen months. He was formerly employed at Messrs. Reynolds's Tannery, and later by Messrs. Peter Walker and Son, Ltd. Always of a cheery nature, he was well respected by all with Whom he came in contact. PRIVATE J. CURLEY. Lady Loses Husband And Child Simultaneously. PRIVATE J. CURLEY. Died of Wounds.

John Curley, of the Lancashire Fusiliers, died of wounds On the 10th inst. It was only last week that a comrade wrote saying he escorted Private Curley to the dressing station, he having been wounded in the arm. Private Curley, who was 29 years of age, had been in the Army since the first month of the war and. bad served in France for the past twelve months. He was -.employed before en- listing "by Messrs. Williams, sculptors. He was educated at St. Mary's R.C. School, Buttermaaket-street.. sad feature of the cue is that the morning following the receipt of the news of her hus- band's death Mrs. Curley lost, through death, a five-year-old child. She• has two other 'children.

MISSING. PRIVATE FL DMSLEY. One With Nine Years' Service Reported Missing. Private Hugh Daisley, of the Lancashire Fusiliers;, whose home address is 130, Forster- street, is reported missing. • His mother re- ceived word from the War Office on Tuesday, whilst a letter has also been received from an officer stating that he has not seen Private Daisley since a recent battle in which he took part. • Private Daisley, who is 30. years of age, served in the Army for nine years and on the Reserve for three years, and was called up immediately on the Outbreak of war. He was employed by Messrs: Jos. Crosfield and Sons,

CRICKET. WARRINGTON AN XI. V. ST. HELENS RECS. Played last Saturday on the fine enclosure belong- ing to Pilkington's Works at St. Helens, there being a large crowd present and ideal Weather pre- vailing. The visitors were minus the services of most of their regular players, who were otherwise engaged, while several others who promised to assist, failed to make an appearance. Naturally, the visitors paid the penalty for going with a weak side, but they deserve some credit for fulfilling this fixture in the circumstances. They received the thanks of the Si. Helens Recs. for their enthusiasm. The Recs. :took full advantage of the weak oppo- sition, and although they lost J. E. Walker early on, J. Tabern (130 not out) and W. Shaw (78) made a great stand of 186 Apr the second wicket. Both players gave chancesNVhich were not accepted, but when they settled down, they fairly punished the Warrington bowling. W. Southward (32) also gave a bright display, having seven boundaries in his total. For the visitors the batting honours were earned by R. Stephens (11), G. Moore (12), T. Carrington (11), and G. Duckworth (13). The last-named opened the innings and was seventh out at fifty— batting in correct style. He received a fine ovation from both players and spectators. 3, Constable (captain) was getting nicely set when he was dis- missed by a brilliant catch, while Lemon, who has been showing good form this season, failed to score. J. Tabern (6 wickets for 25 runs) and J. Addison (4 wickets for 17 runs) were the most successful bowlers. This is Warrington's first , defeat this season, but they hope to make a better show when the return fixture is played on August 12th. Scores St. Helens Recs.—J. Tabern not out 130, J. E. Walker lbw b Allen 4, W. Shaw b Tole 78, W. Southward b Constable 32, A. Addison not out 5. extras 19; total (for 3 wickets, innings declared) 268. W. Starkey, 3. Addison, H. Addison, 13; Brooks, W. Baines, P Eaves did not bat. Warrington An Xl.—G. Duckworth c Eaves b Tabern 13, T. E. Allen b Tabern 2, G. Moore c Shaw b Tabern 12. F. Lemon c Eaves b Tabern 0, T. Carrington b J. Addison 11, F. Tole c Starkey b Tabern 1. F. Parr c Eaves b Addison 4, J. Con- stable c Harrison b Addison 7, B. Taylor at. Eaves b Addison 5, P. Ward not out 2, R. Stephens c Brooks b Tabern 11, extras 7; total 75. • TO-DAY'S MATCHES. WARRINGTON XI. V. J. P. PRESTON'S PRESCOT XI. This match will be played at Prescot to-day, com- mencing at 3 p.m. Warrington XI. Pro3pects For Coming Season At Wilderspool. 11111 A meeting of the Committee of the Warrington Rugby Football Club was held on Tussday. Mr. E. Osborne (president) in the chair. Mr. T. Bunchi was appointed chairman of the committee. A vote of condolence was passed with the wi-Joy of the late George Thomas. killed in action, and the Secretary (Mr. T. P. Bradshaw) was instructed to forward her the resolution. The annual meeting of the Northern Union will be held at Huddersfield next Tuesday, when the question of the continuance of football next season will be considered. A meeting of the N.U. League will follow. The Warrington Club will be represented at the meetings, so that early next week we shall know whether or not Wilderspool is to see Warrington operating during the coming winter. It is intended in any case to run an Intermediqte League Competition again, and an effort w'll bE made to arrange matches with Leagues belonging. to other towns. WHITE HEATHER DAY. A bowling carnival is to be held on the Arples Bowling Ground on August 26th, when amateur teams of three aside will be allowed to compete. Valuable prizes will 11e given to winners and run- ners up.
